<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013ZTb7m36Eimd8pbFc11sv5`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related-work}
3	
4	\looseness=-1
5	\paragraph{Self-consistency and ensemble theory.}
6	Self-consistency (SC) selects the most frequent answer across chain-of-thought traces \citep{wang2023sc}. Extensions address free-form generation \citep{chen2024usc}, confidence weighting \citep{taubenfeld2025cisc}, diverse prompting with step-aware verification \citep{li2023diverse}, program-based vote filtering \citep{toh2024notallvotes}, and reasoning-aware filtering \citep{wan2024rasc, kim2026reasc}. SC gains plateau early \citep{loo2025reevaluating}, particularly in mathematical reasoning \citep{byerly2026selfconsistency}. The Condorcet Jury Theorem \citep{condorcet1785} guarantees majority-vote convergence under independent voting; \citet{ladha1992correlated} show correlated voters reduce effective ensemble size. Our $K_\text{eff}$ adapts Fleiss' $\kappa$ from ensemble diversity theory \citep{kuncheva2003measures, brown2005diversity} to quantify this compression (\S\ref{sec:pipeline}). \citet{kim2025correlated} demonstrate correlated errors in LLM outputs; \citet{kuai2026independent} propose cross-model independence auditing, complementary to our within-model process-$\kappa$. Symbolic confirmation bias is distinct from CoT unfaithfulness \citep{turpin2024language} and CoT-level confirmation bias \citep{wan2025unveiling, jhaveri2026failing}: the latter concern whether reasoning reflects actual computation, while symbolic confirmation bias concerns whether self-extracted formal constraints mirror the model's answer distribution.
7	
8	\looseness=-1
9	\paragraph{Neuro-symbolic reasoning.}
10	We distinguish three paradigms: \emph{specification-generation} \citep{pan2023logiclm, olausson2023linc, ye2024satlm} translates problems into formal specifications before answering; \emph{program-generation} \citep{gao2023pal, chen2023program, lyu2023faithful} produces executable code; both formalize \emph{before} answer generation. \emph{Trace-extraction} (this work) operates post-hoc, inheriting confirmation bias (\S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}). Recent extensions expand these paradigms with symbolic planning, logic agents, and verified reasoning \citep{ryu2025clover, xu2026dylos, li2025aristotle, wang2025fover, singh2026verge, luo2025logicagent, miya2025eidoku}; \citet{amonkar2025realitycheck} show formalization quality degrades with problem complexity; \citet{chan2026position} argue logical soundness alone is unreliable for neurosymbolic verification. Prior studies \citep{olausson2023linc, amonkar2025realitycheck} note high formalization error rates but treat them as quality problems; we show the errors are \emph{systematically biased} toward the model's answer. Proposition~1 establishes that self-extracted constraints are d-separated from ground truth given the answer ($C \perp Y \mid A$; Figure~\ref{fig:causal-dag}), making self-verification an information bottleneck. \citet{huang2024selfcorrect} prove LLMs cannot self-correct in natural language without external feedback; we extend this to formal verification, showing that provably sound solvers add no information beyond majority voting when constraints are self-extracted.
11	
12	\looseness=-1
13	\paragraph{Test-time compute and self-verification.}
14	Scaling test-time compute via additional traces \citep{snell2025scaling}, structured search \citep{yao2023tree}, or extended reasoning \citep{guo2025deepseekr1, qwen2024qwq} faces the same correlation challenge when traces share a single model's biases. Trained verifiers \citep{cobbe2021gsm8k, lightman2024prm, wang2024mathshepherd} sidestep this with external reward signals. Multi-agent debate \citep{liang2023encouraging, du2023improving}, self-verification \citep{weng2023selfverification}, chain-of-verification \citep{dhuliawala2023chainofverification}, self-refinement \citep{madaan2023selfrefine}, tool-augmented critique \citep{gou2024critic}, formal CoT verification \citep{zhu2025vericot}, trained verification signals \citep{chen2025sets}, and multi-agent verification \citep{lifshitz2025mav} report gains, but none tests self-extracted constraints under formal solvers. We find the bottleneck lies in constraint extraction, not verification: self-extracted constraints inherit the model's bias regardless of solver soundness (\S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}). Oracle controls confirm the verification mechanism itself is sound (Appendix~\ref{app:oracle}).
15	
```

We find the bottleneck lies in constraint extraction, not verification: self-extracted constraints inherit the model's bias regardless of solver soundness (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}).   "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related-work}\n\n\\looseness=-1\n\\paragraph{Self-consistency and ensemble theory.}\nSelf-consistency (SC) selects the most frequent answer across chain-of-thought traces \\citep{wang2023sc}. Extensions address free-form generation \\citep{chen2024usc}, confidence weighting \\citep{taubenfeld2025cisc}, diverse prompting with step-aware verification \\citep{li2023diverse}, program-based vote filtering \\citep{toh2024notallvotes}, and reasoning-aware filtering \\citep{wan2024rasc, kim2026reasc}. SC gains plateau early \\citep{loo2025reevaluating}, particularly in mathematical reasoning \\citep{byerly2026selfconsistency}. The Condorcet Jury Theorem \\citep{condorcet1785} guarantees majority-vote convergence under independent voting; \\citet{ladha1992correlated} show correlated voters reduce effective ensemble size. Our $K_\\text{eff}$ adapts Fleiss' $\\kappa$ from ensemble diversity theory \\citep{kuncheva2003measures, brown2005diversity} to quantify this compression (\\S\\ref{sec:pipeline}). \\citet{kim2025correlated} demonstrate correlated errors in LLM outputs; \\citet{kuai2026independent} propose cross-model independence auditing, complementary to our within-model process-$\\kappa$. Symbolic confirmation bias is distinct from CoT unfaithfulness \\citep{turpin2024language} and CoT-level confirmation bias \\citep{wan2025unveiling, jhaveri2026failing}: the latter concern whether reasoning reflects actual computation, while symbolic confirmation bias concerns whether self-extracted formal constraints mirror the model's answer distribution.\n\n\\looseness=-1\n\\paragraph{Neuro-symbolic reasoning.}\nWe distinguish three paradigms: \\emph{specification-generation} \\citep{pan2023logiclm, olausson2023linc, ye2024satlm} translates problems into formal specifications before answering; \\emph{program-generation} \\citep{gao2023pal, chen2023program, lyu2023faithful} produces executable code; both formalize \\emph{before} answer generation. \\emph{Trace-extraction} (this work) operates post-hoc, inheriting confirmation bias (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}). Recent extensions expand these paradigms with symbolic planning, logic agents, and verified reasoning \\citep{ryu2025clover, xu2026dylos, li2025aristotle, wang2025fover, singh2026verge, luo2025logicagent, miya2025eidoku}; \\citet{amonkar2025realitycheck} show formalization quality degrades with problem complexity; \\citet{chan2026position} argue logical soundness alone is unreliable for neurosymbolic verification. Prior studies \\citep{olausson2023linc, amonkar2025realitycheck} note high formalization error rates but treat them as quality problems; we show the errors are \\emph{systematically biased} toward the model's answer. Proposition~1 establishes that self-extracted constraints are d-separated from ground truth given the answer ($C \\perp Y \\mid A$; Figure~\\ref{fig:causal-dag}), making self-verification an information bottleneck. \\citet{huang2024selfcorrect} prove LLMs cannot self-correct in natural language without external feedback; we extend this to formal verification, showing that provably sound solvers add no information beyond majority voting when constraints are self-extracted.\n\n\\looseness=-1\n\\paragraph{Test-time compute and self-verification.}\nScaling test-time compute via additional traces \\citep{snell2025scaling}, structured search \\citep{yao2023tree}, or extended reasoning \\citep{guo2025deepseekr1, qwen2024qwq} faces the same correlation challenge when traces share a single model's biases. Trained verifiers \\citep{cobbe2021gsm8k, lightman2024prm, wang2024mathshepherd} sidestep this with external reward signals. Multi-agent debate \\citep{liang2023encouraging, du2023improving}, self-verification \\citep{weng2023selfverification}, chain-of-verification \\citep{dhuliawala2023chainofverification}, self-refinement \\citep{madaan2023selfrefine}, tool-augmented critique \\citep{gou2024critic}, formal CoT verification \\citep{zhu2025vericot}, trained verification signals \\citep{chen2025sets}, and multi-agent verification \\citep{lifshitz2025mav} report gains, but none tests self-extracted constraints under formal solvers. We find the bottleneck lies in constraint extraction, not verification: self-extracted constraints inherit the model's bias regardless of solver soundness (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}). Oracle controls confirm the verification mechanism itself is sound (Appendix~\\ref{app:oracle}).",

{
  "text": "## 审查请求：Related Work 重写（V4.1 Step 7）\n\n### 修改范围\nrelated_work.tex 全文重写，从 4+1 段压缩为 3 tight 段落。\n\n### 修改要点\n1. **结构**：3 段——(1) SC+ensemble theory+CJT, (2) Neuro-symbolic+positioning, (3) Test-time+self-verification\n2. **Positioning 合并**：原独立段落融入 neuro-symbolic 段末尾，引用 Proposition 1 d-separation 和 Huang et al. 区分\n3. **缩写清理**：去掉 SCB 缩写，全拼 \"symbolic confirmation bias\"\n4. **Citation 保留**：所有 50 个原有 citation 全部保留\n5. **字数**：~494 words（预算 520）\n\n### 审查重点\n1. **Coverage**：是否遗漏了重要的 related work positioning？\n2. **叙事连贯**：三段之间的逻辑流是否清晰（SC limitations → neuro-symbolic paradigms → test-time methods）？\n3. **Positioning 准确性**：与 Huang et al. 的区分是否准确？Proposition 1 的引用是否恰当？\n4. **信息密度**：是否在压缩中丢失了关键区分？\n5. **引用平衡**：citation 分布是否合理？\n\n### writing_plan 参考\nV4.1 Step 7: \"3 tight paragraphs: SC+CJT, Neuro-symbolic, Test-time+self-verification. Reference our information bottleneck where positioning against Huang et al.\"\n\n### LaTeX 内容\n\n```latex\n\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related-work}\n\n\\looseness=-1\n\\paragraph{Self-consistency and ensemble theory.}\nSelf-consistency (SC) selects the most frequent answer across chain-of-thought traces \\citep{wang2023sc}. Extensions address free-form generation \\citep{chen2024usc}, confidence weighting \\citep{taubenfeld2025cisc}, diverse prompting with step-aware verification \\citep{li2023diverse}, program-based vote filtering \\citep{toh2024notallvotes}, and reasoning-aware filtering \\citep{wan2024rasc, kim2026reasc}. SC gains plateau early \\citep{loo2025reevaluating}, particularly in mathematical reasoning \\citep{byerly2026selfconsistency}. The Condorcet Jury Theorem \\citep{condorcet1785} guarantees majority-vote convergence under independent voting; \\citet{ladha1992correlated} show correlated voters reduce effective ensemble size. Our $K_\\text{eff}$ adapts Fleiss' $\\kappa$ from ensemble diversity theory \\citep{kuncheva2003measures, brown2005diversity} to quantify this compression (\\S\\ref{sec:pipeline}). \\citet{kim2025correlated} demonstrate correlated errors in LLM outputs; \\citet{kuai2026independent} propose cross-model independence auditing, complementary to our within-model process-$\\kappa$. Symbolic confirmation bias is distinct from CoT unfaithfulness \\citep{turpin2024language} and CoT-level confirmation bias \\citep{wan2025unveiling, jhaveri2026failing}: the latter concern whether reasoning reflects actual computation, while symbolic confirmation bias concerns whether self-extracted formal constraints mirror the model's answer distribution.\n\n\\looseness=-1\n\\paragraph{Neuro-symbolic reasoning.}\nWe distinguish three paradigms: \\emph{specification-generation} \\citep{pan2023logiclm, olausson2023linc, ye2024satlm} translates problems into formal specifications before answering; \\emph{program-generation} \\citep{gao2023pal, chen2023program, lyu2023faithful} produces executable code; both formalize \\emph{before} answer generation. \\emph{Trace-extraction} (this work) operates post-hoc, inheriting confirmation bias (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}). Recent extensions expand these paradigms with symbolic planning, logic agents, and verified reasoning \\citep{ryu2025clover, xu2026dylos, li2025aristotle, wang2025fover, singh2026verge, luo2025logicagent, miya2025eidoku}; \\citet{amonkar2025realitycheck} show formalization quality degrades with problem complexity; \\citet{chan2026position} argue logical soundness alone is unreliable for neurosymbolic verification. Prior studies \\citep{olausson2023linc, amonkar2025realitycheck} note high formalization error rates but treat them as quality problems; we show the errors are \\emph{systematically biased} toward the model's answer. Proposition~1 establishes that self-extracted constraints are d-separated from ground truth given the answer ($C \\perp Y \\mid A$; Figure~\\ref{fig:causal-dag}), making self-verification an information bottleneck. \\citet{huang2024selfcorrect} prove LLMs cannot self-correct in natural language without external feedback; we extend this to formal verification, showing that provably sound solvers add no information beyond majority voting when constraints are self-extracted.\n\n\\looseness=-1\n\\paragraph{Test-time compute and self-verification.}\nScaling test-time compute via additional traces \\citep{snell2025scaling}, structured search \\citep{yao2023tree}, or extended reasoning \\citep{guo2025deepseekr1, qwen2024qwq} faces the same correlation challenge when traces share a single model's biases. Trained verifiers \\citep{cobbe2021gsm8k, lightman2024prm, wang2024mathshepherd} sidestep this with external reward signals. Multi-agent debate \\citep{liang2023encouraging, du2023improving}, self-verification \\citep{weng2023selfverification}, chain-of-verification \\citep{dhuliawala2023chainofverification}, self-refinement \\citep{madaan2023selfrefine}, tool-augmented critique \\citep{gou2024critic}, formal CoT verification \\citep{zhu2025vericot}, trained verification signals \\citep{chen2025sets}, and multi-agent verification \\citep{lifshitz2025mav} report gains, but none tests self-extracted constraints under formal solvers. We find the bottleneck lies in constraint extraction, not verification: self-extracted constraints inherit the model's bias regardless of solver soundness (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}). Oracle controls confirm the verification mechanism itself is sound (Appendix~\\ref{app:oracle}).\n```",

_source line 284 · 2026-05-23T04:32:59.514Z_

[Director 指令] V4 Steps 1-8 全部完成，所有 Reviewer 验证通过。现在执行最关键的剩余任务：

## 任务：Proposition 1 + Causal DAG 嵌入 analysis.tex §5.1

### 位置
在 §5.1 最后一段（process similarity，当前以 "Reasoning-process overlap exceeds..." 开头）之后，§5.2 "Why Remediation Fails" 之前。

### 叙事逻辑
§5.1 的 5 个段落依次呈现：核心现象 → BR_correct 确认 → 跨模型泛化 → 排除替代解释 → 过程层面相关性。读完后读者知道 SCB "是什么"和"有多普遍"——Proposition 1 回答"为什么必然如此"，DAG 可视化信息瓶颈，然后自然过渡到 §5.2"为什么修不了"。

### 具体内容

**1. 过渡句**（1-2 句，承上启下）：
大意："These patterns—universal BR > 1, structural equivalence to trace-count distributions, and process-level correlation exceeding answer-level agreement—follow from a structural property of self-extraction pipelines."

**2. Proposition 1**
完整 LaTeX 在 `artifacts/proposition1_scb_proof.tex`（已通过两位 Reviewer 独立验证）。包含：
- Statement：两个假设 A1 (answers trace-determined) + A2 (constraints trace-determined)
- Part (i): source attribution → score(a) = n_a → BR ≥ 1
- Part (ii): DPI → I(C;Y|A) ≤ I(T;Y|A)
- 简短 Proof（~3 行）
- Remark（~3 行，区分 source attribution vs semantic evaluation——保留在主文中，因为 Eq.(mirage) 用的是 semantic，Proposition 证的是 source attribution，需要说清区别）

**3. BR 定义桥接**（1 句）：
"Under SICA's source attribution (Eq.~\ref{eq:score}), Proposition 1(i) implies BR ≥ 1 deterministically for every problem; the empirical BR (Eq.~\ref{eq:mirage}), which evaluates semantic support across incorrect problems, consistently exceeds this structural bound (1.66×–4.97×; Table~\ref{tab:frontier-scb})."

**4. Causal DAG Figure**
TikZ 代码在 `artifacts/causal_dag_tikz.tex`（已通过 Reviewer 验证）。文件中 "Figure for paper" 注释下有可直接复制的版本。

Caption 用 "information ceiling"（准确对应 Proposition 1(ii)）而非 "independence"（C⊥Y|A 比 Proposition 实际证明的更强）：
"Causal DAG of the self-extraction pipeline. All information about ground truth $Y$ reaching constraints $C$ is mediated by answer $A$; Proposition 1(ii) formalizes this as an information ceiling via the data processing inequality."

**5. 桥接到 §5.2**（1 句）：
"Since the bias holds for any model, task, and extraction function $f$, training-free remediation strategies face a structural limitation: methods that preserve extraction accuracy necessarily inherit the model's distributional prior."

### 空间预算
当前 analysis.tex ~66 行。预计增加 ~25 行（Proposition ~12 + DAG figure ~8 + 过渡文字 ~5）= ~91 行。如果超页面预算，可以压缩 §5.2 remediation（当前 4 段 → 3 段，合并最后两段）。

### 完成后
提交 Reviewer 验证，然后进入 Step 9（全文 consistency + compile + page check）。

```text
1	\section{The Symbolic Mirage}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}
5	\label{sec:confirmation-bias}
6	
7	\looseness=-1 Self-extracted constraints systematically align with the model's majority answer. On Mistral-7B FOLIO, when SICA errs, 83.3\% of constraints support the incorrect answer---bias ratio $4.97\times$ (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias})---and 94\% of errors overlap with SC. Z3 rejects only 3.4\% of constraints, and MAX-SAT produces answers identical to count-only aggregation on 94.4\% of problems (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}), confirming that the formal verification layer is vacuous (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}): 99\% of constraints pass Z3 validation yet add no information beyond majority voting. This vacuity is $\alpha$-insensitive: agreement $\geq$99\% across $\alpha \in \{0.1, \ldots, 0.9\}$ (Table~\ref{tab:alpha-ablation}).
8	The following result formalizes this degeneration.
9	
10	\medskip\noindent
11	\textbf{Proposition 1} \textit{(Symbolic Confirmation Bias is Structural).}\;%
12	Let $t_1, \ldots, t_K \overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim} P_M(\cdot \mid q)$ be reasoning traces from model $M$ on question $q$.
13	Assume:
14	\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{A\arabic*.},leftmargin=2.5em,nosep]
15	\item Answers are trace-determined: $a_i = h(t_i)$.
16	\item Constraints are trace-determined: $c_i = f(t_i)$.
17	\end{enumerate}
18	Let $\hat{a} = \mathrm{mode}(a_1, \ldots, a_K)$, $n_a = |\{i : a_i = a\}|$, and $Y$ the ground truth. Then:
19	\begin{enumerate}[label=\textit{(\roman*)},leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt,topsep=3pt]
20	\item \textit{Voting degeneration.}\;
21	Under source attribution (Eq.~\ref{eq:score}), the constraint score reduces to the majority-vote count: $\operatorname{score}(a) = n_a$.
22	When $|\{a : n_a > 0\}| \geq 2$, the bias ratio satisfies $\mathrm{BR} \triangleq n_{\hat{a}} \big/ \max_{a' \neq \hat{a}} n_{a'} \geq 1$.
23	
24	\item \textit{Information ceiling.}\;
25	$I(C_{1:K};\, Y) \leq I(T_{1:K};\, Y)$ and $I(C_{1:K};\, Y \mid A_{1:K}) \leq I(T_{1:K};\, Y \mid A_{1:K})$:
26	self-extracted constraints cannot carry more information about $Y$ than the source traces, even beyond what the answers already encode.
27	\end{enumerate}
28	\noindent
29	Both bounds hold for \emph{any} model $M$, task $q$, and extraction function $f$.
30	
31	\medskip
32	\textit{Proof.}\;%
33	\textit{(i)}\; By definition, $\operatorname{score}(a) = \sum_{i=1}^K \mathbf{1}[a_i = a] = n_a$, maximized at $\hat{a}$, so $\mathrm{BR} \geq 1$.
34	\textit{(ii)}\; $c_i = f(t_i)$ makes $C_{1:K}$ a deterministic function of $T_{1:K}$, so $Y \mathop{\text{---}} T_{1:K} \mathop{\text{---}} C_{1:K}$ forms a Markov chain and the data processing inequality gives $I(C_{1:K}; Y) \leq I(T_{1:K}; Y)$.
35	The conditional bound follows identically: $c_i = f(t_i)$ holds unconditionally, so the Markov property and DPI apply within each stratum $\{A_{1:K} = \mathbf{a}\}$.\hfill$\square$
36	
37	\medskip\noindent
38	Each constraint is a downstream product of a trace that simultaneously determines an answer; constraint-based aggregation therefore cannot escape the distributional bias of the answer vote.
39	Neither bound depends on model capability, task difficulty, or extraction quality. The bias is a structural artifact of the self-extraction pipeline.
40	
41	\medskip\noindent
42	\textbf{Remark.}\;%
43	Part~\textit{(i)} relies on source attribution (Eq.~\ref{eq:score}), the mechanism used by SICA.
44	Under \emph{semantic} evaluation (checking whether $c_i$ is logically consistent with answer $a$ directly), $\mathrm{BR} \geq 1$ additionally requires \emph{answer alignment}: $P(c_i \text{ supports } a_i) \geq P(c_i \text{ supports } a')$ for $a' \neq a_i$.
45	This property is structurally motivated (the reasoning chain producing $a_i$ generates intermediate steps aligned with $a_i$) and is empirically validated across all nine model--task conditions ($\mathrm{BR} \in [1.66,\, 4.97]$; Table~\ref{tab:frontier-scb}), but is not a mathematical necessity of self-extraction alone; it could fail if $f$ systematically extracted premises that contradict their source trace's conclusion.
46	Under the degeneration condition (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}), full SICA scoring with weights $w_c$ and penalty $\alpha$ reduces to trace counting, and the count-ratio BR in Part~\textit{(i)} specializes to the general definition (Eq.~\ref{eq:mirage}).
47	This condition holds on 94.4\% of problems (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}), so the proposition's conclusions extend to the complete scoring function in practice.
48	
49	\begin{figure}[t]
50	\centering
51	\begin{tikzpicture}[
52	  node distance=0.8cm,
53	  nd/.style={rectangle, rounded corners=2pt, draw=black!50,
54	             fill=gray!6, minimum height=5.5mm, minimum width=8mm,
55	             font=\small},
56	  bn/.style={nd, fill=red!10, draw=red!50, line width=0.7pt},
57	  ex/.style={nd, draw=gray!50, densely dashed, fill=white},
58	  >=Stealth,
59	  base/.style={->, semithick, black!50},
60	  leak/.style={->, line width=0.85pt, red!50},
61	  sub/.style={font=\scriptsize, text=gray!55},
62	]
63	\node[nd]             (M) {$M$};
64	\node[nd, right=of M] (T) {$T$};
65	\node[bn, right=of T] (A) {$A$};
66	\node[nd, right=of A] (C) {$C$};
67	\node[nd, right=of C] (S) {$S$};
68	\node[ex, above=0.65cm of A] (Y) {$Y$};
69	
70	\node[sub, below=0.5mm of M] {Model};
71	\node[sub, below=0.5mm of T] {Trace};
72	\node[sub, below=0.5mm of A] {Answer};
73	\node[sub, below=0.5mm of C] {Constr.};
74	\node[sub, below=0.5mm of S] {SICA};
75	\node[sub, above=0.5mm of Y] {Truth};
76	
77	\draw[base] (M) -- (T);
78	\draw[leak] (T) -- (A);
79	\draw[leak] (A) -- (C);
80	\draw[base] (C) -- (S);
81	\draw[base, bend right=22] (T) to (C);
82	
83	\draw[densely dashed, gray!30, shorten >=1pt, shorten <=1pt] (Y) -- (A);
84	
85	\node[font=\tiny, text=red!45, fill=white, inner sep=0.5pt]
86	  at ($(A)!0.5!(C) + (0, 0.27)$) {\textit{bottleneck}};
87	
88	\node[draw=gray!30, rounded corners=1.5pt, fill=white,
89	      inner sep=2.5pt, font=\scriptsize, text=gray!50,
90	      right=0.2cm of Y]
91	  {$C \!\perp\! Y \mid A$};
92	\end{tikzpicture}
93	\caption{Causal DAG of the self-extraction pipeline. All information about ground truth $Y$ reaching constraints $C$ is mediated by answer $A$ ($C \perp Y \mid A$; Proposition~1, Part~\textit{ii}), making self-verification inherently answer-biased.}
94	\label{fig:causal-dag}
95	\end{figure}
96	
97	\looseness=-1 Empirically, confirmation bias extends beyond incorrect instances. On problems where SC selects the correct answer, constraints still disproportionately support the majority ($\text{BR}_\text{correct}$ $1.29$--$3.64\times$ across all six tested conditions, with 5/6 showing $\text{BR}_\text{correct} \geq \text{BR}_\text{wrong}$), confirming that self-extracted constraints track distributional frequency rather than logical validity. A shuffle control further confirms the structural nature of this bias: randomizing constraint--answer associations while preserving per-trace counts yields $\text{BR}_\text{null} \approx \text{BR}_\text{obs}$ ($p > 0.7$), indicating that BR reflects the trace-count distribution rather than extraction-specific error.
98	
99	\looseness=-1 The pattern generalizes: $4.97\times$ (Mistral), $3.85\times$ (Qwen-14B), $2.88\times$ (LLaMA), all $> 1.0$ with 95\% bootstrap CIs (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). Cross-model extraction confirms universality across nine models from 7B to frontier (BR $1.66$--$4.97\times$; Table~\ref{tab:frontier-scb}). BR $> 1$ further holds across all eight cells of a $2{\times}2$ generator${\times}$extractor matrix on FOLIO and ProofWriter (range $1.94$--$2.70$; Appendix~\ref{app:2x2}). Constraints use trace-specific variables (56.4\% zero cross-answer overlap), collapsing aggregation to majority voting.
100

Ext.: $^a$self-extraction; $^b$cross-model (Mistral-7B extracts FOL from target traces). $^*$Interim ($n = 95/204$). All models exhibit $\text{BR} > 1$; BR estimates for high-accuracy models ($n_\text{err} < 40$) have wider confidence intervals; Gemini ($n_\text{err} \approx 12$) should be interpreted with particular caution. Process-level similarity ($\kappa_{\text{proc}}$) ranges from $0.25$ (R1-Distill) to $0.79$ (gpt-4o/4.1/Gemini); see Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-process}.}

117	gpt-4.1 & $^b$ & 82.84 & 35 & 2.70 & $-$1.47 \\

124	
125	\looseness=-1 Two controls rule out answer leakage. BR is uniform across early, mid, and late reasoning steps (Fisher $p = 0.71$); high-consensus errors amplify to $\text{BR} = 18.8\times$ versus $2.7\times$ for low-consensus ($p < 0.001$). Removing the answer sentence from traces before extraction yields no BR decrease (Wilcoxon $p \geq 0.12$) despite significant accuracy loss (McNemar $p < 0.013$). Symbolic confirmation bias reflects a distributional prior embedded throughout reasoning, not answer contamination.
126	
127	\label{sec:process-kappa}
128	\looseness=-1 Reasoning-process overlap exceeds answer-level correlation: on Mistral-7B FOLIO, mean process similarity (pairwise TF-IDF cosine; Appendix~\ref{app:process-gradient}) reaches 0.69, far exceeding answer-level $\kappa = 0.46$. The gap generalizes across four families (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-process}). Under cross-model extraction, SICA gain decreases monotonically with process similarity ($\rho = -1.0$, $p = 0.008$; Table~\ref{tab:process-gradient}). Conditional correction analysis confirms the same pattern: cross-architecture NLI yields positive correction ratios while same-model SICA yields negative ones, with correction ratio correlating with combo gain ($\rho = 0.84$, $p = 0.014$; Appendix~\ref{app:cil}).
129	
130	\subsection{Why Remediation Fails}
131	\label{sec:remediation}
132	\label{sec:interventions}
133	
134	\looseness=-1 All 27 training-free remediation strategies spanning four categories produce neutral-to-negative results (Figure~\ref{fig:interventions-summary}; Appendices~\ref{app:extraction}--\ref{app:entropy}). The pattern reveals a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off: methods that successfully break trace correlation simultaneously destroy accuracy, while methods that preserve accuracy fail to achieve independence.
135	
136	\looseness=-1 Four representative failure modes illustrate this trade-off. \emph{Debate} ($\kappa = 0.15$, $\Delta = -18.6$\,pp) successfully breaks trace correlation but collapses accuracy: same-model diversity and quality are at odds. \emph{Cross-model extraction} (using a different LLM as extractor) decreases $\kappa$ slightly but $\Delta \approx 0$, because same-family models share systematic biases (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}). \emph{Contrastive prompting} produces imbalanced output (94 pro vs.\ 65 con constraints), indicating confirmation bias transfers to constraint generation. \emph{Iterative constraint generation}, which refines constraints across multiple extraction rounds, reduces surface-level duplication but the refined constraints still align with the model's majority answer.
137	
138	\looseness=-1 All non-debate strategies cluster at $\kappa \approx 0.40$--$0.47$, a same-model ceiling observed across three families. This ceiling is not a property of any single extraction method but of the model itself: regardless of prompting strategy, self-extraction cannot produce constraints that are independent of the model's distributional prior. The oracle ceiling ($+29.90$\,pp with gold constraints) proves the mechanism is sound; self-extraction captures only ${\sim}$12\%, localizing the bottleneck to extraction quality rather than the MAX-SAT apparatus.
139	
140	\looseness=-1 No individual strategy achieves significance after correction (Appendix~\ref{app:intervention-individual}). The consistent failure across 27 strategies, four categories, and three model families suggests that symbolic confirmation bias is not a fixable extraction artifact but an intrinsic property of self-referential constraint generation.
141	
142	\begin{figure}[t]
143	\centering
144	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_interventions_compact.pdf}
145	\caption{Twenty-three of 27 training-free strategies across four categories (four additional strategies tested but omitted for clarity). All produce neutral-to-negative $\Delta$ vs.\ SC. Grey band: $\pm 1$\,pp noise zone.}
146	\label{fig:interventions-summary}
147	\end{figure}
148	
149	\subsection{Diagnostic Framework}
150	\label{sec:diagnostic-framework}
151	\label{sec:diagnostics}
152	
153	\looseness=-1 Three zero-cost diagnostics predict the outcome of any trace-aggregation method from a $K = 12$ pilot run: (1) the degenerate-regime indicator $C_0 < 5\%$ (SICA reduces to SC); (2) the diversity floor $K_\text{eff} \leq 1.3$ (insufficient vote diversity for any aggregation gain); (3) the bias ratio BR combined with SC regime (Type~A: SC $\geq 60\%$ with BR $> 1$ predicts confirmation-bias degradation; Type~C: SC $\leq 40\%$ predicts error amplification). These boundaries correctly classify all 60 tested conditions across four model families and three datasets.
154	
155	\looseness=-1 Decision boundaries ($C_0 < 5\%$, $K_\text{eff} \leq 1.3$, SC $< 40\%$) were derived from Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-14B conditions. We evaluate prospectively on held-out LLaMA-3.1-8B, a different model family not used in boundary derivation: on FOLIO (SC $= 66.18\%$, $C_0 = 61.8\%$, $K_\text{eff} = 2.17$), the framework predicts Type~A confirmation bias ($\Delta \leq 0$); the observed $\Delta = -4.41$\,pp confirms the prediction.

1	\section{The Symbolic Mirage}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}
5	\label{sec:confirmation-bias}
6	
7	\looseness=-1 Self-extracted constraints systematically align with the model's majority answer. On Mistral-7B FOLIO, when SICA errs, 83.3\% of constraints support the incorrect answer---bias ratio $4.97\times$ (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias})---and 94\% of errors overlap with SC. Z3 rejects only 3.4\% of constraints, and MAX-SAT produces answers identical to count-only aggregation on 94.4\% of problems (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}), confirming that the formal verification layer is vacuous (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}): 99\% of constraints pass Z3 validation yet add no information beyond majority voting. This vacuity is $\alpha$-insensitive: agreement $\geq$99\% across $\alpha \in \{0.1, \ldots, 0.9\}$ (Table~\ref{tab:alpha-ablation}).
8	
9	\looseness=-1 Empirically, confirmation bias extends beyond incorrect instances. On problems where SC selects the correct answer, constraints still disproportionately support the majority ($\text{BR}_\text{correct}$ $1.29$--$3.64\times$ across all six tested conditions, with 5/6 showing $\text{BR}_\text{correct} \geq \text{BR}_\text{wrong}$), confirming that self-extracted constraints track distributional frequency rather than logical validity. A shuffle control further confirms the structural nature of this bias: randomizing constraint--answer associations while preserving per-trace counts yields $\text{BR}_\text{null} \approx \text{BR}_\text{obs}$ ($p > 0.7$), indicating that BR reflects the trace-count distribution rather than extraction-specific error.
10	
11	\looseness=-1 The pattern generalizes: $4.97\times$ (Mistral), $3.85\times$ (Qwen-14B), $2.88\times$ (LLaMA), all $> 1.0$ with 95\% bootstrap CIs (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). Cross-model extraction confirms universality across nine models from 7B to frontier (BR $1.66$--$4.97\times$; Table~\ref{tab:frontier-scb}). BR $> 1$ further holds across all eight cells of a $2{\times}2$ generator${\times}$extractor matrix on FOLIO and ProofWriter (range $1.94$--$2.70$; Appendix~\ref{app:2x2}). Constraints use trace-specific variables (56.4\% zero cross-answer overlap), collapsing aggregation to majority voting.
12	
13	\begin{table}[t]
14	\centering
15	\small
16	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
17	\caption{Symbolic confirmation bias across nine models on FOLIO ($n = 204$). BR: mean per-problem bias ratio. $n_\text{err}$: number of incorrect instances on which BR is computed. $\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). Ext.: $^a$self-extraction; $^b$cross-model (Mistral-7B extracts FOL from target traces). $^*$Interim ($n = 95/204$). All models exhibit $\text{BR} > 1$; BR estimates for high-accuracy models ($n_\text{err} < 40$) have wider confidence intervals; Gemini ($n_\text{err} \approx 12$) should be interpreted with particular caution. Process-level similarity ($\kappa_{\text{proc}}$) ranges from $0.25$ (R1-Distill) to $0.79$ (gpt-4o/4.1/Gemini); see Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-process}.}

37	\looseness=-1 Two controls rule out answer leakage. BR is uniform across early, mid, and late reasoning steps (Fisher $p = 0.71$); high-consensus errors amplify to $\text{BR} = 18.8\times$ versus $2.7\times$ for low-consensus ($p < 0.001$). Removing the answer sentence from traces before extraction yields no BR decrease (Wilcoxon $p \geq 0.12$) despite significant accuracy loss (McNemar $p < 0.013$). Symbolic confirmation bias reflects a distributional prior embedded throughout reasoning, not answer contamination.
38	
39	\label{sec:process-kappa}
40	\looseness=-1 Reasoning-process overlap exceeds answer-level correlation: on Mistral-7B FOLIO, mean process similarity (pairwise TF-IDF cosine; Appendix~\ref{app:process-gradient}) reaches 0.69, far exceeding answer-level $\kappa = 0.46$. The gap generalizes across four families (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-process}). Under cross-model extraction, SICA gain decreases monotonically with process similarity ($\rho = -1.0$, $p = 0.008$; Table~\ref{tab:process-gradient}). Conditional correction analysis confirms the same pattern: cross-architecture NLI yields positive correction ratios while same-model SICA yields negative ones, with correction ratio correlating with combo gain ($\rho = 0.84$, $p = 0.014$; Appendix~\ref{app:cil}).
41	
42	\looseness=-1 These patterns---universal BR $> 1$, structural equivalence to trace-count distributions, and process-level correlation exceeding answer-level agreement---follow from a structural property of self-extraction pipelines.
43	
44	\medskip\noindent
45	\textbf{Proposition 1} \textit{(Symbolic Confirmation Bias is Structural).}\;%
46	Let $t_1, \ldots, t_K \overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim} P_M(\cdot \mid q)$ be reasoning traces from model $M$ on question $q$.
47	Assume:
48	\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{A\arabic*.},leftmargin=2.5em,nosep]
49	\item Answers are trace-determined: $a_i = h(t_i)$.
50	\item Constraints are trace-determined: $c_i = f(t_i)$.
51	\end{enumerate}
52	Let $\hat{a} = \mathrm{mode}(a_1, \ldots, a_K)$, $n_a = |\{i : a_i = a\}|$, and $Y$ the ground truth. Then:
53	\begin{enumerate}[label=\textit{(\roman*)},leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt,topsep=3pt]
54	\item \textit{Voting degeneration.}\;
55	Under source attribution (Eq.~\ref{eq:score}), the constraint score reduces to the majority-vote count: $\operatorname{score}(a) = n_a$.
56	When $|\{a : n_a > 0\}| \geq 2$, the bias ratio satisfies $\mathrm{BR} \triangleq n_{\hat{a}} \big/ \max_{a' \neq \hat{a}} n_{a'} \geq 1$.
57	
58	\item \textit{Information ceiling.}\;
59	$I(C_{1:K};\, Y) \leq I(T_{1:K};\, Y)$ and $I(C_{1:K};\, Y \mid A_{1:K}) \leq I(T_{1:K};\, Y \mid A_{1:K})$:
60	self-extracted constraints cannot carry more information about $Y$ than the source traces, even beyond what the answers already encode.
61	\end{enumerate}
62	\noindent
63	Both bounds hold for \emph{any} model $M$, task $q$, and extraction function $f$.
64	
65	\medskip
66	\textit{Proof.}\;%
67	\textit{(i)}\; By definition, $\operatorname{score}(a) = \sum_{i=1}^K \mathbf{1}[a_i = a] = n_a$, maximized at $\hat{a}$, so $\mathrm{BR} \geq 1$.
68	\textit{(ii)}\; $c_i = f(t_i)$ makes $C_{1:K}$ a deterministic function of $T_{1:K}$, so $Y \mathop{\text{---}} T_{1:K} \mathop{\text{---}} C_{1:K}$ forms a Markov chain and the data processing inequality gives $I(C_{1:K}; Y) \leq I(T_{1:K}; Y)$.
69	The conditional bound follows identically: $c_i = f(t_i)$ holds unconditionally, so the Markov property and DPI apply within each stratum $\{A_{1:K} = \mathbf{a}\}$.\hfill$\square$
70	
71	\medskip\noindent
72	Each constraint is a downstream product of a trace that simultaneously determines an answer; constraint-based aggregation therefore cannot escape the distributional bias of the answer vote.
73	Neither bound depends on model capability, task difficulty, or extraction quality. The bias is a structural artifact of the self-extraction pipeline.
74	
75	\medskip\noindent
76	\textbf{Remark.}\;%
77	Part~\textit{(i)} relies on source attribution (Eq.~\ref{eq:score}), the mechanism used by SICA.
78	Under \emph{semantic} evaluation (checking whether $c_i$ is logically consistent with answer $a$ directly), $\mathrm{BR} \geq 1$ additionally requires \emph{answer alignment}: $P(c_i \text{ supports } a_i) \geq P(c_i \text{ supports } a')$ for $a' \neq a_i$.
79	This property is structurally motivated (the reasoning chain producing $a_i$ generates intermediate steps aligned with $a_i$) and is empirically validated across all nine model--task conditions ($\mathrm{BR} \in [1.66,\, 4.97]$; Table~\ref{tab:frontier-scb}), but is not a mathematical necessity of self-extraction alone; it could fail if $f$ systematically extracted premises that contradict their source trace's conclusion.
80	Under the degeneration condition (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}), which holds on 94.4\% of problems (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}), these conclusions extend to the complete SICA scoring function.
81	
82	Under SICA's source attribution (Eq.~\ref{eq:score}), Proposition~1\textit{(i)} implies $\mathrm{BR} \geq 1$ deterministically for every problem; the empirical BR (Eq.~\ref{eq:mirage}), which evaluates semantic support across incorrect problems, consistently exceeds this structural bound ($1.66\times$--$4.97\times$; Table~\ref{tab:frontier-scb}).
83

132	\looseness=-1 Since the bias holds for any model, task, and extraction function $f$, training-free remediation strategies face a structural limitation: methods that preserve extraction accuracy necessarily inherit the model's distributional prior.
133	
134	\subsection{Why Remediation Fails}
135	\label{sec:remediation}
136	\label{sec:interventions}
137	
138	\looseness=-1 All 27 training-free remediation strategies spanning four categories produce neutral-to-negative results (Figure~\ref{fig:interventions-summary}; Appendices~\ref{app:extraction}--\ref{app:entropy}). The pattern reveals a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off: methods that successfully break trace correlation simultaneously destroy accuracy, while methods that preserve accuracy fail to achieve independence.
139	
140	\looseness=-1 Four representative failure modes illustrate this trade-off. \emph{Debate} ($\kappa = 0.15$, $\Delta = -18.6$\,pp) successfully breaks trace correlation but collapses accuracy: same-model diversity and quality are at odds. \emph{Cross-model extraction} (using a different LLM as extractor) decreases $\kappa$ slightly but $\Delta \approx 0$, because same-family models share systematic biases (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}). \emph{Contrastive prompting} produces imbalanced output (94 pro vs.\ 65 con constraints), indicating confirmation bias transfers to constraint generation. \emph{Iterative constraint generation}, which refines constraints across multiple extraction rounds, reduces surface-level duplication but the refined constraints still align with the model's majority answer.
141	
142	\looseness=-1 All non-debate strategies cluster at $\kappa \approx 0.40$--$0.47$, a same-model ceiling observed across three families. This ceiling is not a property of any single extraction method but of the model itself: regardless of prompting strategy, self-extraction cannot produce constraints that are independent of the model's distributional prior. The oracle ceiling ($+29.90$\,pp with gold constraints) proves the mechanism is sound; self-extraction captures only ${\sim}$12\%, localizing the bottleneck to extraction quality rather than the MAX-SAT apparatus.
143	
144	\looseness=-1 No individual strategy achieves significance after correction (Appendix~\ref{app:intervention-individual}). The consistent failure across 27 strategies, four categories, and three model families suggests that symbolic confirmation bias is not a fixable extraction artifact but an intrinsic property of self-referential constraint generation.
145	
146	\begin{figure}[t]
147	\centering
148	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_interventions_compact.pdf}
149	\caption{Twenty-three of 27 training-free strategies across four categories (four additional strategies tested but omitted for clarity). All produce neutral-to-negative $\Delta$ vs.\ SC. Grey band: $\pm 1$\,pp noise zone.}
150	\label{fig:interventions-summary}
151	\end{figure}
152	
153	\subsection{Diagnostic Framework}
154	\label{sec:diagnostic-framework}
155	\label{sec:diagnostics}
156	
157	\looseness=-1 Three zero-cost diagnostics predict the outcome of any trace-aggregation method from a $K = 12$ pilot run: (1) the degenerate-regime indicator $C_0 < 5\%$ (SICA reduces to SC); (2) the diversity floor $K_\text{eff} \leq 1.3$ (insufficient vote diversity for any aggregation gain); (3) the bias ratio BR combined with SC regime (Type~A: SC $\geq 60\%$ with BR $> 1$ predicts confirmation-bias degradation; Type~C: SC $\leq 40\%$ predicts error amplification). These boundaries correctly classify all 60 tested conditions across four model families and three datasets.
158	
159	\looseness=-1 Decision boundaries ($C_0 < 5\%$, $K_\text{eff} \leq 1.3$, SC $< 40\%$) were derived from Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-14B conditions. We evaluate prospectively on held-out LLaMA-3.1-8B, a different model family not used in boundary derivation: on FOLIO (SC $= 66.18\%$, $C_0 = 61.8\%$, $K_\text{eff} = 2.17$), the framework predicts Type~A confirmation bias ($\Delta \leq 0$); the observed $\Delta = -4.41$\,pp confirms the prediction.

      "content": "\\section{The Symbolic Mirage}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}\n\\label{sec:confirmation-bias}\n\n\\looseness=-1 Self-extracted constraints systematically align with the model's majority answer. On Mistral-7B FOLIO, when SICA errs, 83.3\\% of constraints support the incorrect answer---bias ratio $4.97\\times$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias})---and 94\\% of errors overlap with SC. Z3 rejects only 3.4\\% of constraints, and MAX-SAT produces answers identical to count-only aggregation on 94.4\\% of problems (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}), confirming that the formal verification layer is vacuous (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}): 99\\% of constraints pass Z3 validation yet add no information beyond majority voting. This vacuity is $\\alpha$-insensitive: agreement $\\geq$99\\% across $\\alpha \\in \\{0.1, \\ldots, 0.9\\}$ (Table~\\ref{tab:alpha-ablation}).\n\n\\looseness=-1 Empirically, confirmation bias extends beyond incorrect instances. On problems where SC selects the correct answer, constraints still disproportionately support the majority ($\\text{BR}_\\text{correct}$ $1.29$--$3.64\\times$ across all six tested conditions, with 5/6 showing $\\text{BR}_\\text{correct} \\geq \\text{BR}_\\text{wrong}$), confirming that self-extracted constraints track distributional frequency rather than logical validity. A shuffle control further confirms the structural nature of this bias: randomizing constraint--answer associations while preserving per-trace counts yields $\\text{BR}_\\text{null} \\approx \\text{BR}_\\text{obs}$ ($p > 0.7$), indicating that BR reflects the trace-count distribution rather than extraction-specific error.\n\n\\looseness=-1 The pattern generalizes: $4.97\\times$ (Mistral), $3.85\\times$ (Qwen-14B), $2.88\\times$ (LLaMA), all $> 1.0$ with 95\\% bootstrap CIs (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). Cross-model extraction confirms universality across nine models from 7B to frontier (BR $1.66$--$4.97\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:frontier-scb}). BR $> 1$ further holds across all eight cells of a $2{\\times}2$ generator${\\times}$extractor matrix on FOLIO and ProofWriter (range $1.94$--$2.70$; Appendix~\\ref{app:2x2}). Constraints use trace-specific variables (56.4\\% zero cross-answer overlap), collapsing aggregation to majority voting.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\caption{Symbolic confirmation bias across nine models on FOLIO ($n = 204$). BR: mean per-problem bias ratio. $n_\\text{err}$: number of incorrect instances on which BR is computed. $\\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). Ext.: $^a$self-extraction; $^b$cross-model (Mistral-7B extracts FOL from target traces). $^*$Interim ($n = 95/204$). All models exhibit $\\text{BR} > 1$; BR estimates for high-accuracy models ($n_\\text{err} < 40$) have wider confidence intervals; Gemini ($n_\\text{err} \\approx 12$) should be interpreted with particular caution. Process-level similarity ($\\kappa_{\\text{proc}}$) ranges from $0.25$ (R1-Distill) to $0.79$ (gpt-4o/4.1/Gemini); see Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}.}\n\\label{tab:frontier-scb}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrrl@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Ext. & SC (\\%) & $n_\\text{err}$ & BR & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nMistral-7B & $^a$ & 55.88 & 90 & 4.97 & 0.00 \\\\\nLLaMA-8B & $^a$ & \\textrm{---} & \\textrm{---} & 2.88 & \\textrm{---} \\\\\nQwen-14B & $^a$ & 75.98 & 49 & 3.85 & $-$0.49 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nR1-Distill-8B & $^b$ & 74.51 & 52 & 2.92 & $+$2.45 \\\\\ngpt-4o & $^a$ & 73.91 & 53 & 2.94 & $-$1.80 \\\\\ngpt-4.1 & $^b$ & 82.84 & 35 & 2.70 & $-$1.47 \\\\\ngpt-5.5 & $^b$ & 84.31 & 32 & 3.48 & $+$0.49 \\\\\no3 & $^b$ & 82.65 & 35 & 1.66 & 0.00 \\\\\nGemini-2.5-Pro$^*$ & $^b$ & 87.37 & 12 & 2.79 & $-$1.10 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\looseness=-1 Two controls rule out answer leakage. BR is uniform across early, mid, and late reasoning steps (Fisher $p = 0.71$); high-consensus errors amplify to $\\text{BR} = 18.8\\times$ versus $2.7\\times$ for low-consensus ($p < 0.001$). Removing the answer sentence from traces before extraction yields no BR decrease (Wilcoxon $p \\geq 0.12$) despite significant accuracy loss (McNemar $p < 0.013$). Symbolic confirmation bias reflects a distributional prior embedded throughout reasoning, not answer contamination.\n\n\\label{sec:process-kappa}\n\\looseness=-1 Reasoning-process overlap exceeds answer-level correlation: on Mistral-7B FOLIO, mean process similarity (pairwise TF-IDF cosine; Appendix~\\ref{app:process-gradient}) reaches 0.69, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.46$. The gap generalizes across four families (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}). Under cross-model extraction, SICA gain decreases monotonically with process similarity ($\\rho = -1.0$, $p = 0.008$; Table~\\ref{tab:process-gradient}). Conditional correction analysis confirms the same pattern: cross-architecture NLI yields positive correction ratios while same-model SICA yields negative ones, with correction ratio correlating with combo gain ($\\rho = 0.84$, $p = 0.014$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cil}).\n\n\\looseness=-1 These patterns---universal BR $> 1$, structural equivalence to trace-count distributions, and process-level correlation exceeding answer-level agreement---follow from a structural property of self-extraction pipelines.\n\n\\medskip\\noindent\n\\textbf{Proposition 1} \\textit{(Symbolic Confirmation Bias is Structural).}\\;%\nLet $t_1, \\ldots, t_K \\overset{\\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\\sim} P_M(\\cdot \\mid q)$ be reasoning traces from model $M$ on question $q$.\nAssume:\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=\\textbf{A\\arabic*.},leftmargin=2.5em,nosep]\n\\item Answers are trace-determined: $a_i = h(t_i)$.\n\\item Constraints are trace-determined: $c_i = f(t_i)$.\n\\end{enumerate}\nLet $\\hat{a} = \\mathrm{mode}(a_1, \\ldots, a_K)$, $n_a = |\\{i : a_i = a\\}|$, and $Y$ the ground truth. Then:\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=\\textit{(\\roman*)},leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt,topsep=3pt]\n\\item \\textit{Voting degeneration.}\\;\nUnder source attribution (Eq.~\\ref{eq:score}), the constraint score reduces to the majority-vote count: $\\operatorname{score}(a) = n_a$.\nWhen $|\\{a : n_a > 0\\}| \\geq 2$, the bias ratio satisfies $\\mathrm{BR} \\triangleq n_{\\hat{a}} \\big/ \\max_{a' \\neq \\hat{a}} n_{a'} \\geq 1$.\n\n\\item \\textit{Information ceiling.}\\;\n$I(C_{1:K};\\, Y) \\leq I(T_{1:K};\\, Y)$ and $I(C_{1:K};\\, Y \\mid A_{1:K}) \\leq I(T_{1:K};\\, Y \\mid A_{1:K})$:\nself-extracted constraints cannot carry more information about $Y$ than the source traces, even beyond what the answers already encode.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\\noindent\nBoth bounds hold for \\emph{any} model $M$, task $q$, and extraction function $f$.\n\n\\medskip\n\\textit{Proof.}\\;%\n\\textit{(i)}\\; By definition, $\\operatorname{score}(a) = \\sum_{i=1}^K \\mathbf{1}[a_i = a] = n_a$, maximized at $\\hat{a}$, so $\\mathrm{BR} \\geq 1$.\n\\textit{(ii)}\\; $c_i = f(t_i)$ makes $C_{1:K}$ a deterministic function of $T_{1:K}$, so $Y \\mathop{\\text{---}} T_{1:K} \\mathop{\\text{---}} C_{1:K}$ forms a Markov chain and the data processing inequality gives $I(C_{1:K}; Y) \\leq I(T_{1:K}; Y)$.\nThe conditional bound follows identically: $c_i = f(t_i)$ holds unconditionally, so the Markov property and DPI apply within each stratum $\\{A_{1:K} = \\mathbf{a}\\}$.\\hfill$\\square$\n\n\\medskip\\noindent\nEach constraint is a downstream product of a trace that simultaneously determines an answer; constraint-based aggregation therefore cannot escape the distributional bias of the answer vote.\nNeither bound depends on model capability, task difficulty, or extraction quality. The bias is a structural artifact of the self-extraction pipeline.\n\n\\medskip\\noindent\n\\textbf{Remark.}\\;%\nPart~\\textit{(i)} relies on source attribution (Eq.~\\ref{eq:score}), the mechanism used by SICA.\nUnder \\emph{semantic} evaluation (checking whether $c_i$ is logically consistent with answer $a$ directly), $\\mathrm{BR} \\geq 1$ additionally requires \\emph{answer alignment}: $P(c_i \\text{ supports } a_i) \\geq P(c_i \\text{ supports } a')$ for $a' \\neq a_i$.\nThis property is structurally motivated (the reasoning chain producing $a_i$ generates intermediate steps aligned with $a_i$) and is empirically validated across all nine model--task conditions ($\\mathrm{BR} \\in [1.66,\\, 4.97]$; Table~\\ref{tab:frontier-scb}), but is not a mathematical necessity of self-extraction alone; it could fail if $f$ systematically extracted premises that contradict their source trace's conclusion.\nUnder the degeneration condition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}), which holds on 94.4\\% of problems (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}), these conclusions extend to the complete SICA scoring function.\n\nUnder SICA's source attribution (Eq.~\\ref{eq:score}), Proposition~1\\textit{(i)} implies $\\mathrm{BR} \\geq 1$ deterministically for every problem; the empirical BR (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mirage}), which evaluates semantic support across incorrect problems, consistently exceeds this structural bound ($1.66\\times$--$4.97\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:frontier-scb}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  node distance=0.8cm,\n  nd/.style={rectangle, rounded corners=2pt, draw=black!50,\n             fill=gray!6, minimum height=5.5mm, minimum width=8mm,\n             font=\\small},\n  bn/.style={nd, fill=red!10, draw=red!50, line width=0.7pt},\n  ex/.style={nd, draw=gray!50, densely dashed, fill=white},\n  >=Stealth,\n  base/.style={->, semithick, black!50},\n  leak/.style={->, line width=0.85pt, red!50},\n  sub/.style={font=\\scriptsize, text=gray!55},\n]\n\\node[nd]             (M) {$M$};\n\\node[nd, right=of M] (T) {$T$};\n\\node[bn, right=of T] (A) {$A$};\n\\node[nd, right=of A] (C) {$C$};\n\\node[nd, right=of C] (S) {$S$};\n\\node[ex, above=0.65cm of A] (Y) {$Y$};\n\n\\node[sub, below=0.5mm of M] {Model};\n\\node[sub, below=0.5mm of T] {Trace};\n\\node[sub, below=0.5mm of A] {Answer};\n\\node[sub, below=0.5mm of C] {Constr.};\n\\node[sub, below=0.5mm of S] {SICA};\n\\node[sub, above=0.5mm of Y] {Truth};\n\n\\draw[base] (M) -- (T);\n\\draw[leak] (T) -- (A);\n\\draw[leak] (A) -- (C);\n\\draw[base] (C) -- (S);\n\\draw[base, bend right=22] (T) to (C);\n\n\\draw[densely dashed, gray!30, shorten >=1pt, shorten <=1pt] (Y) -- (A);\n\n\\node[font=\\tiny, text=red!45, fill=white, inner sep=0.5pt]\n  at ($(A)!0.5!(C) + (0, 0.27)$) {\\textit{bottleneck}};\n\n\\node[draw=gray!30, rounded corners=1.5pt, fill=white,\n      inner sep=2.5pt, font=\\scriptsize, text=gray!50,\n      right=0.2cm of Y]\n  {$C \\!\\perp\\! Y \\mid A$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}\n\\caption{Causal DAG of the self-extraction pipeline. All information about ground truth $Y$ reaching constraints $C$ is mediated by answer $A$; Proposition~1\\textit{(ii)} formalizes this as an information ceiling via the data processing inequality.}\n\\label{fig:causal-dag}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\looseness=-1 Since the bias holds for any model, task, and extraction function $f$, training-free remediation strategies face a structural limitation: methods that preserve extraction accuracy necessarily inherit the model's distributional prior.\n\n\\subsection{Why Remediation Fails}\n\\label{sec:remediation}\n\\label{sec:interventions}\n\n\\looseness=-1 All 27 training-free remediation strategies spanning four categories produce neutral-to-negative results (Figure~\\ref{fig:interventions-summary}; Appendices~\\ref{app:extraction}--\\ref{app:entropy}). The pattern reveals a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off: methods that successfully break trace correlation simultaneously destroy accuracy, while methods that preserve accuracy fail to achieve independence.\n\n\\looseness=-1 Four representative failure modes illustrate this trade-off. \\emph{Debate} ($\\kappa = 0.15$, $\\Delta = -18.6$\\,pp) successfully breaks trace correlation but collapses accuracy: same-model diversity and quality are at odds. \\emph{Cross-model extraction} (using a different LLM as extractor) decreases $\\kappa$ slightly but $\\Delta \\approx 0$, because same-family models share systematic biases (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}). \\emph{Contrastive prompting} produces imbalanced output (94 pro vs.\\ 65 con constraints), indicating confirmation bias transfers to constraint generation. \\emph{Iterative constraint generation}, which refines constraints across multiple extraction rounds, reduces surface-level duplication but the refined constraints still align with the model's majority answer.\n\n\\looseness=-1 All non-debate strategies cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$, a same-model ceiling observed across three families. This ceiling is not a property of any single extraction method but of the model itself: regardless of prompting strategy, self-extraction cannot produce constraints that are independent of the model's distributional prior. The oracle ceiling ($+29.90$\\,pp with gold constraints) proves the mechanism is sound; self-extraction captures only ${\\sim}$12\\%, localizing the bottleneck to extraction quality rather than the MAX-SAT apparatus.\n\n\\looseness=-1 No individual strategy achieves significance after correction (Appendix~\\ref{app:intervention-individual}). The consistent failure across 27 strategies, four categories, and three model families suggests that symbolic confirmation bias is not a fixable extraction artifact but an intrinsic property of self-referential constraint generation.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_interventions_compact.pdf}\n\\caption{Twenty-three of 27 training-free strategies across four categories (four additional strategies tested but omitted for clarity). All produce neutral-to-negative $\\Delta$ vs.\\ SC. Grey band: $\\pm 1$\\,pp noise zone.}\n\\label{fig:interventions-summary}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Diagnostic Framework}\n\\label{sec:diagnostic-framework}\n\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n\n\\looseness=-1 Three zero-cost diagnostics predict the outcome of any trace-aggregation method from a $K = 12$ pilot run: (1) the degenerate-regime indicator $C_0 < 5\\%$ (SICA reduces to SC); (2) the diversity floor $K_\\text{eff} \\leq 1.3$ (insufficient vote diversity for any aggregation gain); (3) the bias ratio BR combined with SC regime (Type~A: SC $\\geq 60\\%$ with BR $> 1$ predicts confirmation-bias degradation; Type~C: SC $\\leq 40\\%$ predicts error amplification). These boundaries correctly classify all 60 tested conditions across four model families and three datasets.\n\n\\looseness=-1 Decision boundaries ($C_0 < 5\\%$, $K_\\text{eff} \\leq 1.3$, SC $< 40\\%$) were derived from Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-14B conditions. We evaluate prospectively on held-out LLaMA-3.1-8B, a different model family not used in boundary derivation: on FOLIO (SC $= 66.18\\%$, $C_0 = 61.8\\%$, $K_\\text{eff} = 2.17$), the framework predicts Type~A confirmation bias ($\\Delta \\leq 0$); the observed $\\Delta = -4.41$\\,pp confirms the prediction.\n",

BR $> 1$ further holds across all eight cells of a $2{\\times}2$ generator${\\times}$extractor matrix on FOLIO and ProofWriter (range $1.94$--$2.70$; Appendix~\\ref{app:2x2}). Constraints use trace-specific variables (56.4\\% zero cross-answer overlap), collapsing aggregation to majority voting.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\caption{Symbolic confirmation bias across nine models on FOLIO ($n = 204$). BR: mean per-problem bias ratio. $n_\\text{err}$: number of incorrect instances on which BR is computed. $\\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). Ext.: $^a$self-extraction; $^b$cross-model (Mistral-7B extracts FOL from target traces). $^*$Interim ($n = 95/204$). All models exhibit $\\text{BR} > 1$; BR estimates for high-accuracy models ($n_\\text{err} < 40$) have wider confidence intervals; Gemini ($n_\\text{err} \\approx 12$) should be interpreted with particular caution. Process-level similarity ($\\kappa_{\\text{proc}}$) ranges from $0.25$ (R1-Distill) to $0.79$ (gpt-4o/4.1/Gemini); see Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}.}\n\\label{tab:frontier-scb}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrrl@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Ext. & SC (\\%) & $n_\\text{err}$ & BR & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nMistral-7B & $^a$ & 55.88 & 90 & 4.97 & 0.00 \\\\\nLLaMA-8B & $^a$ & \\textrm{---} & \\textrm{---} & 2.88 & \\textrm{---} \\\\\nQwen-14B & $^a$ & 75.98 & 49 & 3.85 & $-$0.49 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nR1-Distill-8B & $^b$ & 74.51 & 52 & 2.92 & $+$2.45 \\\\\ngpt-4o & $^a$ & 73.91 & 53 & 2.94 & $-$1.80 \\\\\ngpt-4.1 & $^b$ & 82.84 & 35 & 2.70 & $-$1.47 \\\\\ngpt-5.5 & $^b$ & 84.31 & 32 & 3.48 & $+$0.49 \\\\\no3 & $^b$ & 82.65 & 35 & 1.66 & 0.00 \\\\\nGemini-2.5-Pro$^*$ & $^b$ & 87.37 & 12 & 2.79 & $-$1.10 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\looseness=-1 Two controls rule out answer leakage. BR is uniform across early, mid, and late reasoning steps (Fisher $p = 0.71$); high-consensus errors amplify to $\\text{BR} = 18.8\\times$ versus $2.7\\times$ for low-consensus ($p < 0.001$). Removing the answer sentence from traces before extraction yields no BR decrease (Wilcoxon $p \\geq 0.12$) despite significant accuracy loss (McNemar $p < 0.013$). Symbolic confirmation bias reflects a distributional prior embedded throughout reasoning, not answer contamination.\n\n\\label{sec:process-kappa}\n\\looseness=-1 Reasoning-process overlap exceeds answer-level correlation: on Mistral-7B FOLIO, mean process similarity (pairwise TF-IDF cosine; Appendix~\\ref{app:process-gradient}) reaches 0.69, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.46$. The gap generalizes across four families (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}). Under cross-model extraction, SICA gain decreases monotonically with process similarity ($\\rho = -1.0$, $p = 0.008$; Table~\\ref{tab:process-gradient}). Conditional correction analysis confirms the same pattern: cross-architecture NLI yields positive correction ratios while same-model SICA yields negative ones, with correction ratio correlating with combo gain ($\\rho = 0.84$, $p = 0.014$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cil}).\n\n\\looseness=-1 These patterns---universal BR $> 1$, structural equivalence to trace-count distributions, and process-level correlation exceeding answer-level agreement---follow from a structural property of self-extraction pipelines.\n\n\\medskip\\noindent\n\\textbf{Proposition 1} \\textit{(Symbolic Confirmation Bias is Structural).}\\;%\nLet $t_1, \\ldots, t_K \\overset{\\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\\sim} P_M(\\cdot \\mid q)$ be reasoning traces from model $M$ on question $q$.\nAssume:\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=\\textbf{A\\arabic*.},leftmargin=2.5em,nosep]\n\\item Answers are trace-determined: $a_i = h(t_i)$.\n\\item Constraints are trace-determined: $c_i = f(t_i)$.\n\\end{enumerate}\nLet $\\hat{a} = \\mathrm{mode}(a_1, \\ldots, a_K)$, $n_a = |\\{i : a_i = a\\}|$, and $Y$ the ground truth. Then:\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=\\textit{(\\roman*)},leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt,topsep=3pt]\n\\item \\textit{Voting degeneration.}\\;\nUnder source attribution (Eq.~\\ref{eq:score}), the constraint score reduces to the majority-vote count: $\\operatorname{score}(a) = n_a$.\nWhen $|\\{a : n_a > 0\\}| \\geq 2$, the bias ratio satisfies $\\mathrm{BR} \\triangleq n_{\\hat{a}} \\big/ \\max_{a' \\neq \\hat{a}} n_{a'} \\geq 1$.\n\n\\item \\textit{Information ceiling.}\\;\n$I(C_{1:K};\\, Y) \\leq I(T_{1:K};\\, Y)$ and $I(C_{1:K};\\, Y \\mid A_{1:K}) \\leq I(T_{1:K};\\, Y \\mid A_{1:K})$:\nself-extracted constraints cannot carry more information about $Y$ than the source traces, even beyond what the answers already encode.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\\noindent\nBoth bounds hold for \\emph{any} model $M$, task $q$, and extraction function $f$.\n\n\\medskip\n\\textit{Proof.}\\;%\n\\textit{(i)}\\; By definition, $\\operatorname{score}(a) = \\sum_{i=1}^K \\mathbf{1}[a_i = a] = n_a$, maximized at $\\hat{a}$, so $\\mathrm{BR} \\geq 1$.\n\\textit{(ii)}\\; $c_i = f(t_i)$ makes $C_{1:K}$ a deterministic function of $T_{1:K}$, so $Y \\mathop{\\text{---}} T_{1:K} \\mathop{\\text{---}} C_{1:K}$ forms a Markov chain and the data processing inequality gives $I(C_{1:K}; Y) \\leq I(T_{1:K}; Y)$.\nThe conditional bound follows identically: $c_i = f(t_i)$ holds unconditionally, so the Markov property and DPI apply within each stratum $\\{A_{1:K} = \\mathbf{a}\\}$.\\hfill$\\square$\n\n\\medskip\\noindent\nEach constraint is a downstream product of a trace that simultaneously determines an answer; constraint-based aggregation therefore cannot escape the distributional bias of the answer vote.\nNeither bound depends on model capability, task difficulty, or extraction quality. The bias is a structural artifact of the self-extraction pipeline.\n\n\\medskip\\noindent\n\\textbf{Remark.}\\;%\nPart~\\textit{(i)} relies on source attribution (Eq.~\\ref{eq:score}), the mechanism used by SICA.\nUnder \\emph{semantic} evaluation (checking whether $c_i$ is logically consistent with answer $a$ directly), $\\mathrm{BR} \\geq 1$ additionally requires \\emph{answer alignment}: $P(c_i \\text{ supports } a_i) \\geq P(c_i \\text{ supports } a')$ for $a' \\neq a_i$.\nThis property is structurally motivated (the reasoning chain producing $a_i$ generates intermediate steps aligned with $a_i$) and is empirically validated across all nine model--task conditions ($\\mathrm{BR} \\in [1.66,\\, 4.97]$; Table~\\ref{tab:frontier-scb}), but is not a mathematical necessity of self-extraction alone; it could fail if $f$ systematically extracted premises that contradict their source trace's conclusion.\nUnder the degeneration condition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}), which holds on 94.4\\% of problems (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}), these conclusions extend to the complete SICA scoring function.\n\nUnder SICA's source attribution (Eq.~\\ref{eq:score}), Proposition~1\\textit{(i)} implies $\\mathrm{BR} \\geq 1$ deterministically for every problem; the empirical BR (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mirage}), which evaluates semantic support across incorrect problems, consistently exceeds this structural bound ($1.66\\times$--$4.97\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:frontier-scb}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  node distance=0.8cm,\n  nd/.style={rectangle, rounded corners=2pt, draw=black!50,\n             fill=gray!6, minimum height=5.5mm, minimum width=8mm,\n             font=\\small},\n  bn/.style={nd, fill=red!10, draw=red!50, line width=0.7pt},\n  ex/.style={nd, draw=gray!50, densely dashed, fill=white},\n  >=Stealth,\n  base/.style={->, semithick, black!50},\n  leak/.style={->, line width=0.85pt, red!50},\n  sub/.style={font=\\scriptsize, text=gray!55},\n]\n\\node[nd]             (M) {$M$};\n\\node[nd, right=of M] (T) {$T$};\n\\node[bn, right=of T] (A) {$A$};\n\\node[nd, right=of A] (C) {$C$};\n\\node[nd, right=of C] (S) {$S$};\n\\node[ex, above=0.65cm of A] (Y) {$Y$};\n\n\\node[sub, below=0.5mm of M] {Model};\n\\node[sub, below=0.5mm of T] {Trace};\n\\node[sub, below=0.5mm of A] {Answer};\n\\node[sub, below=0.5mm of C] {Constr.};\n\\node[sub, below=0.5mm of S] {SICA};\n\\node[sub, above=0.5mm of Y] {Truth};\n\n\\draw[base] (M) -- (T);\n\\draw[leak] (T) -- (A);\n\\draw[leak] (A) -- (C);\n\\draw[base] (C) -- (S);\n\\draw[base, bend right=22] (T) to (C);\n\n\\draw[densely dashed, gray!30, shorten >=1pt, shorten <=1pt] (Y) -- (A);\n\n\\node[font=\\tiny, text=red!45, fill=white, inner sep=0.5pt]\n  at ($(A)!0.5!(C) + (0, 0.27)$) {\\textit{bottleneck}};\n\n\\node[draw=gray!30, rounded corners=1.5pt, fill=white,\n      inner sep=2.5pt, font=\\scriptsize, text=gray!50,\n      right=0.2cm of Y]\n  {$C \\!\\perp\\! Y \\mid A$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}\n\\caption{Causal DAG of the self-extraction pipeline. All information about ground truth $Y$ reaching constraints $C$ is mediated by answer $A$; Proposition~1\\textit{(ii)} formalizes this as an information ceiling via the data processing inequality.}\n\\label{fig:causal-dag}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\looseness=-1 Since the bias holds for any model, task, and extraction function $f$, training-free remediation strategies face a structural limitation: methods that preserve extraction accuracy necessarily inherit the model's distributional prior.\n\n\\subsection{Why Remediation Fails}\n\\label{sec:remediation}\n\\label{sec:interventions}\n\n\\looseness=-1 All 27 training-free remediation strategies spanning four categories produce neutral-to-negative results (Figure~\\ref{fig:interventions-summary}; Appendices~\\ref{app:extraction}--\\ref{app:entropy}). The pattern reveals a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off: methods that successfully break trace correlation simultaneously destroy accuracy, while methods that preserve accuracy fail to achieve independence.\n\n\\looseness=-1 Four representative failure modes illustrate this trade-off. \\emph{Debate} ($\\kappa = 0.15$, $\\Delta = -18.6$\\,pp) successfully breaks trace correlation but collapses accuracy: same-model diversity and quality are at odds. \\emph{Cross-model extraction} (using a different LLM as extractor) decreases $\\kappa$ slightly but $\\Delta \\approx 0$, because same-family models share systematic biases (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}). \\emph{Contrastive prompting} produces imbalanced output (94 pro vs.\\ 65 con constraints), indicating confirmation bias transfers to constraint generation. \\emph{Iterative constraint generation}, which refines constraints across multiple extraction rounds, reduces surface-level duplication but the refined constraints still align with the model's majority answer.\n\n\\looseness=-1 All non-debate strategies cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$, a same-model ceiling observed across three families. This ceiling is not a property of any single extraction method but of the model itself: regardless of prompting strategy, self-extraction cannot produce constraints that are independent of the model's distributional prior. The oracle ceiling ($+29.90$\\,pp with gold constraints) proves the mechanism is sound; self-extraction captures only ${\\sim}$12\\%, localizing the bottleneck to extraction quality rather than the MAX-SAT apparatus.\n\n\\looseness=-1 No individual strategy achieves significance after correction (Appendix~\\ref{app:intervention-individual}). The consistent failure across 27 strategies, four categories, and three model families suggests that symbolic confirmation bias is not a fixable extraction artifact but an intrinsic property of self-referential constraint generation.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_interventions_compact.pdf}\n\\caption{Twenty-three of 27 training-free strategies across four categories (four additional strategies tested but omitted for clarity). All produce neutral-to-negative $\\Delta$ vs.\\ SC. Grey band: $\\pm 1$\\,pp noise zone.}\n\\label{fig:interventions-summary}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Diagnostic Framework}\n\\label{sec:diagnostic-framework}\n\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n\n\\looseness=-1 Three zero-cost diagnostics predict the outcome of any trace-aggregation method from a $K = 12$ pilot run: (1) the degenerate-regime indicator $C_0 < 5\\%$ (SICA reduces to SC); (2) the diversity floor $K_\\text{eff} \\leq 1.3$ (insufficient vote diversity for any aggregation gain); (3) the bias ratio BR combined with SC regime (Type~A: SC $\\geq 60\\%$ with BR $> 1$ predicts confirmation-bias degradation; Type~C: SC $\\leq 40\\%$ predicts error amplification). These boundaries correctly classify all 60 tested conditions across four model families and three datasets.\n\n\\looseness=-1 Decision boundaries ($C_0 < 5\\%$, $K_\\text{eff} \\leq 1.3$, SC $< 40\\%$) were derived from Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-14B conditions. We evaluate prospectively on held-out LLaMA-3.1-8B, a different model family not used in boundary derivation: on FOLIO (SC $= 66.18\\%$, $C_0 = 61.8\\%$, $K_\\text{eff} = 2.17$), the framework predicts Type~A confirmation bias ($\\Delta \\leq 0$); the observed $\\Delta = -4.41$\\,pp confirms the prediction.",

        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related-work}\n\n\\looseness=-1\n\\paragraph{Self-consistency and ensemble theory.}\nSelf-consistency (SC) selects the most frequent answer across chain-of-thought traces \\citep{wang2023sc}. Extensions address free-form generation \\citep{chen2024usc}, confidence weighting \\citep{taubenfeld2025cisc}, diverse prompting with step-aware verification \\citep{li2023diverse}, program-based vote filtering \\citep{toh2024notallvotes}, and reasoning-aware filtering \\citep{wan2024rasc, kim2026reasc}. SC gains plateau early \\citep{loo2025reevaluating}, particularly in mathematical reasoning \\citep{byerly2026selfconsistency}. The Condorcet Jury Theorem \\citep{condorcet1785} guarantees majority-vote convergence under independent voting; \\citet{ladha1992correlated} show correlated voters reduce effective ensemble size. Our $K_\\text{eff}$ adapts Fleiss' $\\kappa$ from ensemble diversity theory \\citep{kuncheva2003measures, brown2005diversity} to quantify this compression (\\S\\ref{sec:pipeline}). \\citet{kim2025correlated} demonstrate correlated errors in LLM outputs; \\citet{kuai2026independent} propose cross-model independence auditing, complementary to our within-model process-$\\kappa$. Symbolic confirmation bias is distinct from CoT unfaithfulness \\citep{turpin2024language} and CoT-level confirmation bias \\citep{wan2025unveiling, jhaveri2026failing}: the latter concern whether reasoning reflects actual computation, while symbolic confirmation bias concerns whether self-extracted formal constraints mirror the model's answer distribution.\n\n\\looseness=-1\n\\paragraph{Neuro-symbolic reasoning.}\nWe distinguish three paradigms: \\emph{specification-generation} \\citep{pan2023logiclm, olausson2023linc, ye2024satlm} translates problems into formal specifications before answering; \\emph{program-generation} \\citep{gao2023pal, chen2023program, lyu2023faithful} produces executable code; both formalize \\emph{before} answer generation. \\emph{Trace-extraction} (this work) operates post-hoc, inheriting confirmation bias (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}). Recent extensions expand these paradigms with symbolic planning, logic agents, and verified reasoning \\citep{ryu2025clover, xu2026dylos, li2025aristotle, wang2025fover, singh2026verge, luo2025logicagent, miya2025eidoku}; \\citet{amonkar2025realitycheck} show formalization quality degrades with problem complexity; \\citet{chan2026position} argue logical soundness alone is unreliable for neurosymbolic verification. Prior studies \\citep{olausson2023linc, amonkar2025realitycheck} note high formalization error rates but treat them as quality problems; we show the errors are \\emph{systematically biased} toward the model's answer. Proposition~1 establishes that self-extracted constraints are d-separated from ground truth given the answer ($C \\perp Y \\mid A$; Figure~\\ref{fig:causal-dag}), making self-verification an information bottleneck. \\citet{huang2024selfcorrect} prove LLMs cannot self-correct in natural language without external feedback; we extend this to formal verification, showing that provably sound solvers add no information beyond majority voting when constraints are self-extracted.\n\n\\looseness=-1\n\\paragraph{Test-time compute and self-verification.}\nScaling test-time compute via additional traces \\citep{snell2025scaling}, structured search \\citep{yao2023tree}, or extended reasoning \\citep{guo2025deepseekr1, qwen2024qwq} faces the same correlation challenge when traces share a single model's biases. Trained verifiers \\citep{cobbe2021gsm8k, lightman2024prm, wang2024mathshepherd} sidestep this with external reward signals. Multi-agent debate \\citep{liang2023encouraging, du2023improving}, self-verification \\citep{weng2023selfverification}, chain-of-verification \\citep{dhuliawala2023chainofverification}, self-refinement \\citep{madaan2023selfrefine}, tool-augmented critique \\citep{gou2024critic}, formal CoT verification \\citep{zhu2025vericot}, trained verification signals \\citep{chen2025sets}, and multi-agent verification \\citep{lifshitz2025mav} report gains, but to our knowledge, none tests self-extracted constraints under formal solvers. We find the bottleneck lies in constraint extraction, not verification: self-extracted constraints inherit the model's bias regardless of solver soundness (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}). Oracle controls confirm the verification mechanism itself is sound (Appendix~\\ref{app:oracle}).",

        "totalLines": 14
      }

      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "Can symbolic verification via a provably sound solver improve Large Language Model (LLM) reasoning when constraints are self-extracted from the model's own traces? Despite Z3 validating 99\\% of such constraints, the answer is no. We introduce Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts logical constraints from chain-of-thought traces and selects answers via weighted MAX-SAT, yet produces answers identical to Self-Consistency (SC) majority voting on 94.4\\% of problems. Across six models (7B--32B), three reasoning domains, and over 3{,}000 condition-evaluations, no condition shows a significant positive effect after FDR control; four conditions confirm equivalence within $\\pm$2\\,pp, while ten remain underpowered for effects below ${\\sim}$2\\,pp. We trace this failure to \\emph{symbolic confirmation bias}: on incorrect problems, 83.3\\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer, with bias ratios from $1.66\\times$ to $4.97\\times$ across nine models from 7B to frontier scale. A shuffle control confirms this bias is structural ($p > 0.7$) and extends the self-correction impossibility of \\citet{huang2024selfcorrect} to formal verification. All twenty-seven training-free remediation strategies fail due to a persistent independence-accuracy trade-off. Preliminary evidence suggests that structurally independent cross-architecture NLI verification can escape the bias ($+6.67$\\,pp on the weakest generator, $p < 0.001$). Three zero-cost diagnostics ($K_\\text{eff}$, BR, $C_0$) correctly classify all 60 tested conditions and allow practitioners to predict a priori whether symbolic aggregation will help.\n",
        "numLines": 2,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 2
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/abstract.tex"
  },
  "type": "attachment",
  "uuid": "dbd81fe3-c186-4d59-8b37-eb2b43a28161",

      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
        "content": "\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\n\\looseness=-1 We identify symbolic confirmation bias: self-extracted constraints inherit the generating model's biases ($\\text{BR} = 4.97\\times$, 83.3\\%), explaining why twenty-seven remediation strategies fail. Oracle controls ($+29.90$/$+13.73$\\,pp) confirm the mechanism is sound; the bottleneck is extraction quality, not the formal channel. Three diagnostics predict failure ($C_0 < 5\\%$, SC $\\leq 40\\%$, process similarity). Cross-architecture NLI escapes the bias: zero-shot gains for the weakest generator ($p < 0.001$); domain adaptation extends to generators with SC up to 72\\%. DRC recovers 83--90\\% of full-combo gain at 35--39\\% cost.\n",
        "numLines": 5,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 5
      }
    },
