1	\section{The Symbolic Mirage}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}
5	\label{sec:confirmation-bias}
6	
7	\looseness=-1 Self-extracted constraints systematically align with the model's majority answer. On Mistral-7B FOLIO, when SICA errs, 83.3\% of constraints support the incorrect answer---bias ratio $4.97\times$ (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias})---and 94\% of errors overlap with SC. Z3 rejects only 3.4\% of constraints, and MAX-SAT produces answers identical to count-only aggregation on 94.4\% of problems (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}), confirming that the formal verification layer is vacuous (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}): 99\% of constraints pass Z3 validation yet add no information beyond majority voting. This vacuity is $\alpha$-insensitive: agreement $\geq$99\% across $\alpha \in \{0.1, \ldots, 0.9\}$ (Table~\ref{tab:alpha-ablation}).
8	
9	\looseness=-1 Empirically, confirmation bias extends beyond incorrect instances. On problems where SC selects the correct answer, constraints still disproportionately support the majority ($\text{BR}_\text{correct}$ $1.29$--$3.64\times$ across all six tested conditions, with 5/6 showing $\text{BR}_\text{correct} \geq \text{BR}_\text{wrong}$), confirming that self-extracted constraints track distributional frequency rather than logical validity. A shuffle control further confirms the structural nature of this bias: randomizing constraint--answer associations while preserving per-trace counts yields $\text{BR}_\text{null} \approx \text{BR}_\text{obs}$ ($p > 0.7$), indicating that BR reflects the trace-count distribution rather than extraction-specific error.
10	
11	\looseness=-1 The pattern generalizes: $4.97\times$ (Mistral), $3.85\times$ (Qwen-14B), $2.88\times$ (LLaMA), all $> 1.0$ with 95\% bootstrap CIs (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). Cross-model extraction confirms universality across nine models from 7B to frontier (BR $1.66$--$4.97\times$; Table~\ref{tab:frontier-scb}). BR $> 1$ further holds across all eight cells of a $2{\times}2$ generator${\times}$extractor matrix on FOLIO and ProofWriter (range $1.94$--$2.70$; Appendix~\ref{app:2x2}). Constraints use trace-specific variables (56.4\% zero cross-answer overlap), collapsing aggregation to majority voting.
12	
13	\begin{table}[t]
14	\centering
15	\small
16	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
17	\caption{Symbolic confirmation bias across nine models on FOLIO ($n = 204$). BR: mean per-problem bias ratio. $n_\text{err}$: number of incorrect instances on which BR is computed. $\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). Ext.: $^a$self-extraction; $^b$cross-model (Mistral-7B extracts FOL from target traces). $^*$Interim ($n = 95/204$). All models exhibit $\text{BR} > 1$; BR estimates for high-accuracy models ($n_\text{err} < 40$) have wider confidence intervals; Gemini ($n_\text{err} \approx 12$) should be interpreted with particular caution. Process-level similarity ($\kappa_{\text{proc}}$) ranges from $0.25$ (R1-Distill) to $0.79$ (gpt-4o/4.1/Gemini); see Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-process}.}

37	\looseness=-1 Two controls rule out answer leakage. BR is uniform across early, mid, and late reasoning steps (Fisher $p = 0.71$); high-consensus errors amplify to $\text{BR} = 18.8\times$ versus $2.7\times$ for low-consensus ($p < 0.001$). Removing the answer sentence from traces before extraction yields no BR decrease (Wilcoxon $p \geq 0.12$) despite significant accuracy loss (McNemar $p < 0.013$). Symbolic confirmation bias reflects a distributional prior embedded throughout reasoning, not answer contamination.
38	
39	\label{sec:process-kappa}
40	\looseness=-1 Reasoning-process overlap exceeds answer-level correlation: on Mistral-7B FOLIO, mean process similarity (pairwise TF-IDF cosine; Appendix~\ref{app:process-gradient}) reaches 0.69, far exceeding answer-level $\kappa = 0.46$. The gap generalizes across four families (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-process}). Under cross-model extraction, SICA gain decreases monotonically with process similarity ($\rho = -1.0$, $p = 0.008$; Table~\ref{tab:process-gradient}). Conditional correction analysis confirms the same pattern: cross-architecture NLI yields positive correction ratios while same-model SICA yields negative ones, with correction ratio correlating with combo gain ($\rho = 0.84$, $p = 0.014$; Appendix~\ref{app:cil}).
41	
42	\looseness=-1 These patterns---universal BR $> 1$, structural equivalence to trace-count distributions, and process-level correlation exceeding answer-level agreement---follow from a structural property of self-extraction pipelines.
43	
44	\medskip\noindent
45	\textbf{Proposition 1} \textit{(Symbolic Confirmation Bias is Structural).}\;%
46	Let $t_1, \ldots, t_K \overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim} P_M(\cdot \mid q)$ be reasoning traces from model $M$ on question $q$.
47	Assume:
48	\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{A\arabic*.},leftmargin=2.5em,nosep]
49	\item Answers are trace-determined: $a_i = h(t_i)$.
50	\item Constraints are trace-determined: $c_i = f(t_i)$.
51	\end{enumerate}
52	Let $\hat{a} = \mathrm{mode}(a_1, \ldots, a_K)$, $n_a = |\{i : a_i = a\}|$, and $Y$ the ground truth. Then:
53	\begin{enumerate}[label=\textit{(\roman*)},leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt,topsep=3pt]
54	\item \textit{Voting degeneration.}\;
55	Under source attribution (Eq.~\ref{eq:score}), the constraint score reduces to the majority-vote count: $\operatorname{score}(a) = n_a$.
56	When $|\{a : n_a > 0\}| \geq 2$, the bias ratio satisfies $\mathrm{BR} \triangleq n_{\hat{a}} \big/ \max_{a' \neq \hat{a}} n_{a'} \geq 1$.
57	
58	\item \textit{Information ceiling.}\;
59	$I(C_{1:K};\, Y) \leq I(T_{1:K};\, Y)$ and $I(C_{1:K};\, Y \mid A_{1:K}) \leq I(T_{1:K};\, Y \mid A_{1:K})$:
60	self-extracted constraints cannot carry more information about $Y$ than the source traces, even beyond what the answers already encode.
61	\end{enumerate}
62	\noindent
63	Both bounds hold for \emph{any} model $M$, task $q$, and extraction function $f$.
64	
65	\medskip
66	\textit{Proof.}\;%
67	\textit{(i)}\; By definition, $\operatorname{score}(a) = \sum_{i=1}^K \mathbf{1}[a_i = a] = n_a$, maximized at $\hat{a}$, so $\mathrm{BR} \geq 1$.
68	\textit{(ii)}\; $c_i = f(t_i)$ makes $C_{1:K}$ a deterministic function of $T_{1:K}$, so $Y \mathop{\text{---}} T_{1:K} \mathop{\text{---}} C_{1:K}$ forms a Markov chain and the data processing inequality gives $I(C_{1:K}; Y) \leq I(T_{1:K}; Y)$.
69	The conditional bound follows identically: $c_i = f(t_i)$ holds unconditionally, so the Markov property and DPI apply within each stratum $\{A_{1:K} = \mathbf{a}\}$.\hfill$\square$
70	
71	\medskip\noindent
72	Each constraint is a downstream product of a trace that simultaneously determines an answer; constraint-based aggregation therefore cannot escape the distributional bias of the answer vote.
73	Neither bound depends on model capability, task difficulty, or extraction quality. The bias is a structural artifact of the self-extraction pipeline.
74	
75	\medskip\noindent
76	\textbf{Remark.}\;%
77	Part~\textit{(i)} relies on source attribution (Eq.~\ref{eq:score}), the mechanism used by SICA.
78	Under \emph{semantic} evaluation (checking whether $c_i$ is logically consistent with answer $a$ directly), $\mathrm{BR} \geq 1$ additionally requires \emph{answer alignment}: $P(c_i \text{ supports } a_i) \geq P(c_i \text{ supports } a')$ for $a' \neq a_i$.
79	This property is structurally motivated (the reasoning chain producing $a_i$ generates intermediate steps aligned with $a_i$) and is empirically validated across all nine model--task conditions ($\mathrm{BR} \in [1.66,\, 4.97]$; Table~\ref{tab:frontier-scb}), but is not a mathematical necessity of self-extraction alone; it could fail if $f$ systematically extracted premises that contradict their source trace's conclusion.
80	Under the degeneration condition (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}), which holds on 94.4\% of problems (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}), these conclusions extend to the complete SICA scoring function.
81	
82	Under SICA's source attribution (Eq.~\ref{eq:score}), Proposition~1\textit{(i)} implies $\mathrm{BR} \geq 1$ deterministically for every problem; the empirical BR (Eq.~\ref{eq:mirage}), which evaluates semantic support across incorrect problems, consistently exceeds this structural bound ($1.66\times$--$4.97\times$; Table~\ref{tab:frontier-scb}).
83

131	
132	\looseness=-1 Since the bias holds for any model, task, and extraction function $f$, training-free remediation strategies face a structural limitation: methods that preserve extraction accuracy necessarily inherit the model's distributional prior.
133	
134	\subsection{Why Remediation Fails}
135	\label{sec:remediation}
136	\label{sec:interventions}
137	
138	\looseness=-1 All 27 training-free remediation strategies spanning four categories produce neutral-to-negative results (Figure~\ref{fig:interventions-summary}; Appendices~\ref{app:extraction}--\ref{app:entropy}). The pattern reveals a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off: methods that successfully break trace correlation simultaneously destroy accuracy, while methods that preserve accuracy fail to achieve independence.
139	
140	\looseness=-1 Four representative failure modes illustrate this trade-off. \emph{Debate} ($\kappa = 0.15$, $\Delta = -18.6$\,pp) successfully breaks trace correlation but collapses accuracy: same-model diversity and quality are at odds. \emph{Cross-model extraction} (using a different LLM as extractor) decreases $\kappa$ slightly but $\Delta \approx 0$, because same-family models share systematic biases (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}). \emph{Contrastive prompting} produces imbalanced output (94 pro vs.\ 65 con constraints), indicating confirmation bias transfers to constraint generation. \emph{Iterative constraint generation}, which refines constraints across multiple extraction rounds, reduces surface-level duplication but the refined constraints still align with the model's majority answer.
141	
142	\looseness=-1 All non-debate strategies cluster at $\kappa \approx 0.40$--$0.47$, a same-model ceiling observed across three families. This ceiling is not a property of any single extraction method but of the model itself: regardless of prompting strategy, self-extraction cannot produce constraints that are independent of the model's distributional prior. The oracle ceiling ($+29.90$\,pp with gold constraints) proves the mechanism is sound; self-extraction captures only ${\sim}$12\%, localizing the bottleneck to extraction quality rather than the MAX-SAT apparatus.
143	
144	\looseness=-1 No individual strategy achieves significance after correction (Appendix~\ref{app:intervention-individual}). The consistent failure across 27 strategies, four categories, and three model families suggests that symbolic confirmation bias is not a fixable extraction artifact but an intrinsic property of self-referential constraint generation.
145	
146	\begin{figure}[t]
147	\centering
148	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_interventions_compact.pdf}
149	\caption{Twenty-three of 27 training-free strategies across four categories (four additional strategies tested but omitted for clarity). All produce neutral-to-negative $\Delta$ vs.\ SC. Grey band: $\pm 1$\,pp noise zone.}
150	\label{fig:interventions-summary}
151	\end{figure}
152	
153	\subsection{Diagnostic Framework}
154	\label{sec:diagnostic-framework}
155	\label{sec:diagnostics}
156	
157	\looseness=-1 Three zero-cost diagnostics predict the outcome of any trace-aggregation method from a $K = 12$ pilot run: (1) the degenerate-regime indicator $C_0 < 5\%$ (SICA reduces to SC); (2) the diversity floor $K_\text{eff} \leq 1.3$ (insufficient vote diversity for any aggregation gain); (3) the bias ratio BR combined with SC regime (Type~A: SC $\geq 60\%$ with BR $> 1$ predicts confirmation-bias degradation; Type~C: SC $\leq 40\%$ predicts error amplification). These boundaries correctly classify all 60 tested conditions across four model families and three datasets.
158	
159	\looseness=-1 Decision boundaries ($C_0 < 5\%$, $K_\text{eff} \leq 1.3$, SC $< 40\%$) were derived from Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-14B conditions. We evaluate prospectively on held-out LLaMA-3.1-8B, a different model family not used in boundary derivation: on FOLIO (SC $= 66.18\%$, $C_0 = 61.8\%$, $K_\text{eff} = 2.17$), the framework predicts Type~A confirmation bias ($\Delta \leq 0$); the observed $\Delta = -4.41$\,pp confirms the prediction.

Cross-model extraction confirms universality across nine models from 7B to frontier (BR $1.66$--$4.97\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:frontier-scb}). BR $> 1$ further holds across all eight cells of a $2{\\times}2$ generator${\\times}$extractor matrix on FOLIO and ProofWriter (range $1.94$--$2.70$; Appendix~\\ref{app:2x2}). Constraints use trace-specific variables (56.4\\% zero cross-answer overlap), collapsing aggregation to majority voting.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\caption{Symbolic confirmation bias across nine models on FOLIO ($n = 204$). BR: mean per-problem bias ratio. $n_\\text{err}$: number of incorrect instances on which BR is computed. $\\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). Ext.: $^a$self-extraction; $^b$cross-model (Mistral-7B extracts FOL from target traces). $^*$Interim ($n = 95/204$). All models exhibit $\\text{BR} > 1$; BR estimates for high-accuracy models ($n_\\text{err} < 40$) have wider confidence intervals; Gemini ($n_\\text{err} \\approx 12$) should be interpreted with particular caution. Process-level similarity ($\\kappa_{\\text{proc}}$) ranges from $0.25$ (R1-Distill) to $0.79$ (gpt-4o/4.1/Gemini); see Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}.}\n\\label{tab:frontier-scb}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrrl@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Ext. & SC (\\%) & $n_\\text{err}$ & BR & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nMistral-7B & $^a$ & 55.88 & 90 & 4.97 & 0.00 \\\\\nLLaMA-8B & $^a$ & \\textrm{---} & \\textrm{---} & 2.88 & \\textrm{---} \\\\\nQwen-14B & $^a$ & 75.98 & 49 & 3.85 & $-$0.49 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nR1-Distill-8B & $^b$ & 74.51 & 52 & 2.92 & $+$2.45 \\\\\ngpt-4o & $^a$ & 73.91 & 53 & 2.94 & $-$1.80 \\\\\ngpt-4.1 & $^b$ & 82.84 & 35 & 2.70 & $-$1.47 \\\\\ngpt-5.5 & $^b$ & 84.31 & 32 & 3.48 & $+$0.49 \\\\\no3 & $^b$ & 82.65 & 35 & 1.66 & 0.00 \\\\\nGemini-2.5-Pro$^*$ & $^b$ & 87.37 & 12 & 2.79 & $-$1.10 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\looseness=-1 Two controls rule out answer leakage. BR is uniform across early, mid, and late reasoning steps (Fisher $p = 0.71$); high-consensus errors amplify to $\\text{BR} = 18.8\\times$ versus $2.7\\times$ for low-consensus ($p < 0.001$). Removing the answer sentence from traces before extraction yields no BR decrease (Wilcoxon $p \\geq 0.12$) despite significant accuracy loss (McNemar $p < 0.013$). Symbolic confirmation bias reflects a distributional prior embedded throughout reasoning, not answer contamination.\n\n\\label{sec:process-kappa}\n\\looseness=-1 Reasoning-process overlap exceeds answer-level correlation: on Mistral-7B FOLIO, mean process similarity (pairwise TF-IDF cosine; Appendix~\\ref{app:process-gradient}) reaches 0.69, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.46$. The gap generalizes across four families (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}). Under cross-model extraction, SICA gain decreases monotonically with process similarity ($\\rho = -1.0$, $p = 0.008$; Table~\\ref{tab:process-gradient}). Conditional correction analysis confirms the same pattern: cross-architecture NLI yields positive correction ratios while same-model SICA yields negative ones, with correction ratio correlating with combo gain ($\\rho = 0.84$, $p = 0.014$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cil}).\n\n\\looseness=-1 These patterns---universal BR $> 1$, structural equivalence to trace-count distributions, and process-level correlation exceeding answer-level agreement---follow from a structural property of self-extraction pipelines.\n\n\\medskip\\noindent\n\\textbf{Proposition 1} \\textit{(Symbolic Confirmation Bias is Structural).}\\;%\nLet $t_1, \\ldots, t_K \\overset{\\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\\sim} P_M(\\cdot \\mid q)$ be reasoning traces from model $M$ on question $q$.\nAssume:\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=\\textbf{A\\arabic*.},leftmargin=2.5em,nosep]\n\\item Answers are trace-determined: $a_i = h(t_i)$.\n\\item Constraints are trace-determined: $c_i = f(t_i)$.\n\\end{enumerate}\nLet $\\hat{a} = \\mathrm{mode}(a_1, \\ldots, a_K)$, $n_a = |\\{i : a_i = a\\}|$, and $Y$ the ground truth. Then:\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=\\textit{(\\roman*)},leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt,topsep=3pt]\n\\item \\textit{Voting degeneration.}\\;\nUnder source attribution (Eq.~\\ref{eq:score}), the constraint score reduces to the majority-vote count: $\\operatorname{score}(a) = n_a$.\nWhen $|\\{a : n_a > 0\\}| \\geq 2$, the bias ratio satisfies $\\mathrm{BR} \\triangleq n_{\\hat{a}} \\big/ \\max_{a' \\neq \\hat{a}} n_{a'} \\geq 1$.\n\n\\item \\textit{Information ceiling.}\\;\n$I(C_{1:K};\\, Y) \\leq I(T_{1:K};\\, Y)$ and $I(C_{1:K};\\, Y \\mid A_{1:K}) \\leq I(T_{1:K};\\, Y \\mid A_{1:K})$:\nself-extracted constraints cannot carry more information about $Y$ than the source traces, even beyond what the answers already encode.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\\noindent\nBoth bounds hold for \\emph{any} model $M$, task $q$, and extraction function $f$.\n\n\\medskip\n\\textit{Proof.}\\;%\n\\textit{(i)}\\; By definition, $\\operatorname{score}(a) = \\sum_{i=1}^K \\mathbf{1}[a_i = a] = n_a$, maximized at $\\hat{a}$, so $\\mathrm{BR} \\geq 1$.\n\\textit{(ii)}\\; $c_i = f(t_i)$ makes $C_{1:K}$ a deterministic function of $T_{1:K}$, so $Y \\mathop{\\text{---}} T_{1:K} \\mathop{\\text{---}} C_{1:K}$ forms a Markov chain and the data processing inequality gives $I(C_{1:K}; Y) \\leq I(T_{1:K}; Y)$.\nThe conditional bound follows identically: $c_i = f(t_i)$ holds unconditionally, so the Markov property and DPI apply within each stratum $\\{A_{1:K} = \\mathbf{a}\\}$.\\hfill$\\square$\n\n\\medskip\\noindent\nEach constraint is a downstream product of a trace that simultaneously determines an answer; constraint-based aggregation therefore cannot escape the distributional bias of the answer vote.\nNeither bound depends on model capability, task difficulty, or extraction quality. The bias is a structural artifact of the self-extraction pipeline.\n\n\\medskip\\noindent\n\\textbf{Remark.}\\;%\nPart~\\textit{(i)} relies on source attribution (Eq.~\\ref{eq:score}), the mechanism used by SICA.\nUnder \\emph{semantic} evaluation (checking whether $c_i$ is logically consistent with answer $a$ directly), $\\mathrm{BR} \\geq 1$ additionally requires \\emph{answer alignment}: $P(c_i \\text{ supports } a_i) \\geq P(c_i \\text{ supports } a')$ for $a' \\neq a_i$.\nThis property is structurally motivated (the reasoning chain producing $a_i$ generates intermediate steps aligned with $a_i$) and is empirically validated across all nine model--task conditions ($\\mathrm{BR} \\in [1.66,\\, 4.97]$; Table~\\ref{tab:frontier-scb}), but is not a mathematical necessity of self-extraction alone; it could fail if $f$ systematically extracted premises that contradict their source trace's conclusion.\nUnder the degeneration condition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}), which holds on 94.4\\% of problems (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}), these conclusions extend to the complete SICA scoring function.\n\nUnder SICA's source attribution (Eq.~\\ref{eq:score}), Proposition~1\\textit{(i)} implies $\\mathrm{BR} \\geq 1$ deterministically for every problem; the empirical BR (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mirage}), which evaluates semantic support across incorrect problems, consistently exceeds this structural bound ($1.66\\times$--$4.97\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:frontier-scb}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  node distance=0.8cm,\n  nd/.style={rectangle, rounded corners=2pt, draw=black!50,\n             fill=gray!6, minimum height=5.5mm, minimum width=8mm,\n             font=\\small},\n  bn/.style={nd, fill=red!10, draw=red!50, line width=0.7pt},\n  ex/.style={nd, draw=gray!50, densely dashed, fill=white},\n  >=Stealth,\n  base/.style={->, semithick, black!50},\n  leak/.style={->, line width=0.85pt, red!50},\n  sub/.style={font=\\scriptsize, text=gray!55},\n]\n\\node[nd]             (M) {$M$};\n\\node[nd, right=of M] (T) {$T$};\n\\node[bn, right=of T] (A) {$A$};\n\\node[nd, right=of A] (C) {$C$};\n\\node[nd, right=of C] (S) {$S$};\n\\node[ex, above=0.65cm of A] (Y) {$Y$};\n\n\\node[sub, below=0.5mm of M] {Model};\n\\node[sub, below=0.5mm of T] {Trace};\n\\node[sub, below=0.5mm of A] {Answer};\n\\node[sub, below=0.5mm of C] {Constr.};\n\\node[sub, below=0.5mm of S] {SICA};\n\\node[sub, above=0.5mm of Y] {Truth};\n\n\\draw[base] (M) -- (T);\n\\draw[leak] (T) -- (A);\n\\draw[leak] (A) -- (C);\n\\draw[base] (C) -- (S);\n\\draw[base, bend right=22] (T) to (C);\n\n\\draw[densely dashed, gray!30, shorten >=1pt, shorten <=1pt] (Y) -- (A);\n\n\\node[font=\\tiny, text=red!45, fill=white, inner sep=0.5pt]\n  at ($(A)!0.5!(C) + (0, 0.27)$) {\\textit{bottleneck}};\n\n\\node[draw=gray!30, rounded corners=1.5pt, fill=white,\n      inner sep=2.5pt, font=\\scriptsize, text=gray!50,\n      right=0.2cm of Y]\n  {$C \\!\\perp\\! Y \\mid A$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}\n\\caption{Causal DAG of the self-extraction pipeline. All information about ground truth $Y$ reaching constraints $C$ is mediated by answer $A$; Proposition~1\\textit{(ii)} formalizes this as an information ceiling via the data processing inequality.}\n\\label{fig:causal-dag}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\looseness=-1 Since the bias holds for any model, task, and extraction function $f$, training-free remediation strategies face a structural limitation: methods that preserve extraction accuracy necessarily inherit the model's distributional prior.\n\n\\subsection{Why Remediation Fails}\n\\label{sec:remediation}\n\\label{sec:interventions}\n\n\\looseness=-1 All 27 training-free remediation strategies spanning four categories produce neutral-to-negative results (Figure~\\ref{fig:interventions-summary}; Appendices~\\ref{app:extraction}--\\ref{app:entropy}). The pattern reveals a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off: methods that successfully break trace correlation simultaneously destroy accuracy, while methods that preserve accuracy fail to achieve independence.\n\n\\looseness=-1 Four representative failure modes illustrate this trade-off. \\emph{Debate} ($\\kappa = 0.15$, $\\Delta = -18.6$\\,pp) successfully breaks trace correlation but collapses accuracy: same-model diversity and quality are at odds. \\emph{Cross-model extraction} (using a different LLM as extractor) decreases $\\kappa$ slightly but $\\Delta \\approx 0$, because same-family models share systematic biases (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}). \\emph{Contrastive prompting} produces imbalanced output (94 pro vs.\\ 65 con constraints), indicating confirmation bias transfers to constraint generation. \\emph{Iterative constraint generation}, which refines constraints across multiple extraction rounds, reduces surface-level duplication but the refined constraints still align with the model's majority answer.\n\n\\looseness=-1 All non-debate strategies cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$, a same-model ceiling observed across three families. This ceiling is not a property of any single extraction method but of the model itself: regardless of prompting strategy, self-extraction cannot produce constraints that are independent of the model's distributional prior. The oracle ceiling ($+29.90$\\,pp with gold constraints) proves the mechanism is sound; self-extraction captures only ${\\sim}$12\\%, localizing the bottleneck to extraction quality rather than the MAX-SAT apparatus.\n\n\\looseness=-1 No individual strategy achieves significance after correction (Appendix~\\ref{app:intervention-individual}). The consistent failure across 27 strategies, four categories, and three model families suggests that symbolic confirmation bias is not a fixable extraction artifact but an intrinsic property of self-referential constraint generation.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_interventions_compact.pdf}\n\\caption{Twenty-three of 27 training-free strategies across four categories (four additional strategies tested but omitted for clarity). Grey band: $\\pm 1$\\,pp noise zone.}\n\\label{fig:interventions-summary}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Diagnostic Framework}\n\\label{sec:diagnostic-framework}\n\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n\n\\looseness=-1 Three zero-cost diagnostics predict the outcome of any trace-aggregation method from a $K = 12$ pilot run: (1) the degenerate-regime indicator $C_0 < 5\\%$ (SICA reduces to SC); (2) the diversity floor $K_\\text{eff} \\leq 1.3$ (insufficient vote diversity for any aggregation gain); (3) the bias ratio BR combined with SC regime (Type~A: SC $\\geq 60\\%$ with BR $> 1$ predicts confirmation-bias degradation; Type~C: SC $\\leq 40\\%$ predicts error amplification). A shuffle control further confirms the structural nature of this bias: randomizing constraint--answer associations while preserving per-trace counts yields $\\text{BR}_\\text{null} \\approx \\text{BR}_\\text{obs}$ ($p > 0.7$), indicating that BR reflects the trace-count distribution rather than extraction-specific error.\n\n\\looseness=-1 The pattern generalizes: $4.97\\times$ (Mistral), $3.85\\times$ (Qwen-14B), $2.88\\times$ (LLaMA), all $> 1.0$ with 95\\% bootstrap CIs (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). Cross-model extraction confirms universality across nine models from 7B to frontier (BR $1.66$--$4.97\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:frontier-scb}). BR $> 1$ further holds across all eight cells of a $2{\\times}2$ generator${\\times}$extractor matrix on FOLIO and ProofWriter (range $1.94$--$2.70$; Appendix~\\ref{app:2x2}). Constraints use trace-specific variables (56.4\\% zero cross-answer overlap), collapsing aggregation to majority voting.\n\n[Table frontier-scb here]\n\n\\looseness=-1 Two controls rule out answer leakage. BR is uniform across early, mid, and late reasoning steps (Fisher $p = 0.71$); high-consensus errors amplify to $\\text{BR} = 18.8\\times$ versus $2.7\\times$ for low-consensus ($p < 0.001$). Removing the answer sentence from traces before extraction yields no BR decrease (Wilcoxon $p \\geq 0.12$) despite significant accuracy loss (McNemar $p < 0.013$). Symbolic confirmation bias reflects a distributional prior embedded throughout reasoning, not answer contamination.\n\n\\looseness=-1 Reasoning-process overlap exceeds answer-level correlation: on Mistral-7B FOLIO, mean process similarity (pairwise TF-IDF cosine) reaches 0.69, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.46$. [Cross-model + conditional correction details]\n\n\\looseness=-1 These patterns---universal BR $> 1$, structural equivalence to trace-count distributions, and process-level correlation exceeding answer-level agreement---follow from a structural property of self-extraction pipelines.\n\n[PROPOSITION 1 — Symbolic Confirmation Bias is Structural]\n- (i) Voting degeneration: score(a) = n_a, BR ≥ 1\n- (ii) Information ceiling: I(C;Y) ≤ I(T;Y) via DPI\n- Proof included\n- Remark on source attribution vs semantic evaluation\n- Degeneration coverage bridge (94.4% of problems)\n- BR bridge: structural BR ≥ 1 → empirical BR 1.66×–4.97×\n\n[FIGURE 3 — Causal DAG: M→T→A→C→S, C⊥Y|A]\n\nBridge to §5.2: \"Since the bias holds for any model, task, and extraction function f...\"\n\n\\subsection{Why Remediation Fails} [unchanged]\n\\subsection{Diagnostic Framework} [unchanged]\n```\n",
  "project": "symb_invariant_consensus"
}

Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

1	\section{The Symbolic Mirage}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}
5	\label{sec:confirmation-bias}
6	
7	\looseness=-1 Self-extracted constraints systematically align with the model's majority answer. On Mistral-7B FOLIO, when SICA errs, 83.3\% of constraints support the incorrect answer---bias ratio $4.97\times$ (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias})---and 94\% of errors overlap with SC. Z3 rejects only 3.4\% of constraints, and MAX-SAT produces answers identical to count-only aggregation on 94.4\% of problems (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}), confirming that the formal verification layer is vacuous (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}): 99\% of constraints pass Z3 validation yet add no information beyond majority voting. This vacuity is $\alpha$-insensitive: agreement $\geq$99\% across $\alpha \in \{0.1, \ldots, 0.9\}$ (Table~\ref{tab:alpha-ablation}).
8	
9	\looseness=-1 Empirically, confirmation bias extends beyond incorrect instances. On problems where SC selects the correct answer, constraints still disproportionately support the majority ($\text{BR}_\text{correct}$ $1.29$--$3.64\times$ across all six tested conditions, with 5/6 showing $\text{BR}_\text{correct} \geq \text{BR}_\text{wrong}$), confirming that self-extracted constraints track distributional frequency rather than logical validity. A shuffle control further confirms the structural nature of this bias: randomizing constraint--answer associations while preserving per-trace counts yields $\text{BR}_\text{null} \approx \text{BR}_\text{obs}$ ($p > 0.7$), indicating that BR reflects the trace-count distribution rather than extraction-specific error.
10	
11	\looseness=-1 The pattern generalizes: $4.97\times$ (Mistral), $3.85\times$ (Qwen-14B), $2.88\times$ (LLaMA), all $> 1.0$ with 95\% bootstrap CIs (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). Cross-model extraction confirms universality across nine models from 7B to frontier (BR $1.66$--$4.97\times$; Table~\ref{tab:frontier-scb}). BR $> 1$ further holds across all eight cells of a $2{\times}2$ generator${\times}$extractor matrix on FOLIO and ProofWriter (range $1.94$--$2.70$; Appendix~\ref{app:2x2}). Constraints use trace-specific variables (56.4\% zero cross-answer overlap), collapsing aggregation to majority voting.

Removing the answer sentence from traces before extraction yields no BR decrease (Wilcoxon $p \geq 0.12$) despite significant accuracy loss (McNemar $p < 0.013$). Symbolic confirmation bias reflects a distributional prior embedded throughout reasoning, not answer contamination.

39	\label{sec:process-kappa}

The gap generalizes across four families (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-process}). Under cross-model extraction, SICA gain decreases monotonically with process similarity ($\rho = -1.0$, $p = 0.008$; Table~\ref{tab:process-gradient}). Conditional correction analysis confirms the same pattern: cross-architecture NLI yields positive correction ratios while same-model SICA yields negative ones, with correction ratio correlating with combo gain ($\rho = 0.84$, $p = 0.014$; Appendix~\ref{app:cil}).

42	\looseness=-1 These patterns---universal BR $> 1$, structural equivalence to trace-count distributions, and process-level correlation exceeding answer-level agreement---follow from a structural property of self-extraction pipelines.
43	
44	\medskip\noindent
45	\textbf{Proposition 1} \textit{(Symbolic Confirmation Bias is Structural).}\;%
46	Let $t_1, \ldots, t_K \overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim} P_M(\cdot \mid q)$ be reasoning traces from model $M$ on question $q$.
47	Assume:
48	\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{A\arabic*.},leftmargin=2.5em,nosep]
49	\item Answers are trace-determined: $a_i = h(t_i)$.
50	\item Constraints are trace-determined: $c_i = f(t_i)$.
51	\end{enumerate}
52	Let $\hat{a} = \mathrm{mode}(a_1, \ldots, a_K)$, $n_a = |\{i : a_i = a\}|$, and $Y$ the ground truth. Then:
53	\begin{enumerate}[label=\textit{(\roman*)},leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt,topsep=3pt]
54	\item \textit{Voting degeneration.}\;
55	Under source attribution (Eq.~\ref{eq:score}), the constraint score reduces to the majority-vote count: $\operatorname{score}(a) = n_a$.
56	When $|\{a : n_a > 0\}| \geq 2$, the bias ratio satisfies $\mathrm{BR} \triangleq n_{\hat{a}} \big/ \max_{a' \neq \hat{a}} n_{a'} \geq 1$.
57	
58	\item \textit{Information ceiling.}\;
59	$I(C_{1:K};\, Y) \leq I(T_{1:K};\, Y)$ and $I(C_{1:K};\, Y \mid A_{1:K}) \leq I(T_{1:K};\, Y \mid A_{1:K})$:
60	self-extracted constraints cannot carry more information about $Y$ than the source traces, even beyond what the answers already encode.
61	\end{enumerate}
62	\noindent
63	Both bounds hold for \emph{any} model $M$, task $q$, and extraction function $f$.
64	
65	\medskip
66	\textit{Proof.}\;%
67	\textit{(i)}\; By definition, $\operatorname{score}(a) = \sum_{i=1}^K \mathbf{1}[a_i = a] = n_a$, maximized at $\hat{a}$, so $\mathrm{BR} \geq 1$.
68	\textit{(ii)}\; $c_i = f(t_i)$ makes $C_{1:K}$ a deterministic function of $T_{1:K}$, so $Y \mathop{\text{---}} T_{1:K} \mathop{\text{---}} C_{1:K}$ forms a Markov chain and the data processing inequality gives $I(C_{1:K}; Y) \leq I(T_{1:K}; Y)$.
69	The conditional bound follows identically: $c_i = f(t_i)$ holds unconditionally, so the Markov property and DPI apply within each stratum $\{A_{1:K} = \mathbf{a}\}$.\hfill$\square$
70	
71	\medskip\noindent
72	Each constraint is a downstream product of a trace that simultaneously determines an answer; constraint-based aggregation therefore cannot escape the distributional bias of the answer vote.
73	Neither bound depends on model capability, task difficulty, or extraction quality. The bias is a structural artifact of the self-extraction pipeline.
74	
75	\medskip\noindent
76	\textbf{Remark.}\;%
77	Part~\textit{(i)} relies on source attribution (Eq.~\ref{eq:score}), the mechanism used by SICA.
78	Under \emph{semantic} evaluation (checking whether $c_i$ is logically consistent with answer $a$ directly), $\mathrm{BR} \geq 1$ additionally requires \emph{answer alignment}: $P(c_i \text{ supports } a_i) \geq P(c_i \text{ supports } a')$ for $a' \neq a_i$.
79	This property is structurally motivated (the reasoning chain producing $a_i$ generates intermediate steps aligned with $a_i$) and is empirically validated across all nine model--task conditions ($\mathrm{BR} \in [1.66,\, 4.97]$; Table~\ref{tab:frontier-scb}), but is not a mathematical necessity of self-extraction alone; it could fail if $f$ systematically extracted premises that contradict their source trace's conclusion.
80	Under the degeneration condition (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}), which holds on 94.4\% of problems (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}), these conclusions extend to the complete SICA scoring function.
81	
82	Under SICA's source attribution (Eq.~\ref{eq:score}), Proposition~1\textit{(i)} implies $\mathrm{BR} \geq 1$ deterministically for every problem; the empirical BR (Eq.~\ref{eq:mirage}), which evaluates semantic support across incorrect problems, consistently exceeds this structural bound ($1.66\times$--$4.97\times$; Table~\ref{tab:frontier-scb}).
83

132	\looseness=-1 Since the bias holds for any model, task, and extraction function $f$, training-free remediation strategies face a structural limitation: methods that preserve extraction accuracy necessarily inherit the model's distributional prior.
133	
134	\subsection{Why Remediation Fails}
135	\label{sec:remediation}
136	\label{sec:interventions}
137	
138	\looseness=-1 All 27 training-free remediation strategies spanning four categories produce neutral-to-negative results (Figure~\ref{fig:interventions-summary}; Appendices~\ref{app:extraction}--\ref{app:entropy}). The pattern reveals a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off: methods that successfully break trace correlation simultaneously destroy accuracy, while methods that preserve accuracy fail to achieve independence.
139	
140	\looseness=-1 Four representative failure modes illustrate this trade-off. \emph{Debate} ($\kappa = 0.15$, $\Delta = -18.6$\,pp) successfully breaks trace correlation but collapses accuracy: same-model diversity and quality are at odds. \emph{Cross-model extraction} (using a different LLM as extractor) decreases $\kappa$ slightly but $\Delta \approx 0$, because same-family models share systematic biases (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}). \emph{Contrastive prompting} produces imbalanced output (94 pro vs.\ 65 con constraints), indicating confirmation bias transfers to constraint generation. \emph{Iterative constraint generation}, which refines constraints across multiple extraction rounds, reduces surface-level duplication but the refined constraints still align with the model's majority answer.
141	
142	\looseness=-1 All non-debate strategies cluster at $\kappa \approx 0.40$--$0.47$, a same-model ceiling observed across three families. This ceiling is not a property of any single extraction method but of the model itself: regardless of prompting strategy, self-extraction cannot produce constraints that are independent of the model's distributional prior. The oracle ceiling ($+29.90$\,pp with gold constraints) proves the mechanism is sound; self-extraction captures only ${\sim}$12\%, localizing the bottleneck to extraction quality rather than the MAX-SAT apparatus.
143	
144	\looseness=-1 No individual strategy achieves significance after correction (Appendix~\ref{app:intervention-individual}). The consistent failure across 27 strategies, four categories, and three model families suggests that symbolic confirmation bias is not a fixable extraction artifact but an intrinsic property of self-referential constraint generation.
145	
146	\begin{figure}[t]
147	\centering
148	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_interventions_compact.pdf}
149	\caption{Twenty-three of 27 training-free strategies across four categories (four additional strategies tested but omitted for clarity). All produce neutral-to-negative $\Delta$ vs.\ SC. Grey band: $\pm 1$\,pp noise zone.}
150	\label{fig:interventions-summary}
151	\end{figure}
152	
153	\subsection{Diagnostic Framework}
154	\label{sec:diagnostic-framework}
155	\label{sec:diagnostics}
156	
157	\looseness=-1 Three zero-cost diagnostics predict the outcome of any trace-aggregation method from a $K = 12$ pilot run: (1) the degenerate-regime indicator $C_0 < 5\%$ (SICA reduces to SC); (2) the diversity floor $K_\text{eff} \leq 1.3$ (insufficient vote diversity for any aggregation gain); (3) the bias ratio BR combined with SC regime (Type~A: SC $\geq 60\%$ with BR $> 1$ predicts confirmation-bias degradation; Type~C: SC $\leq 40\%$ predicts error amplification). These boundaries correctly classify all 60 tested conditions across four model families and three datasets.
158	
159	\looseness=-1 Decision boundaries ($C_0 < 5\%$, $K_\text{eff} \leq 1.3$, SC $< 40\%$) were derived from Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-14B conditions. We evaluate prospectively on held-out LLaMA-3.1-8B, a different model family not used in boundary derivation: on FOLIO (SC $= 66.18\%$, $C_0 = 61.8\%$, $K_\text{eff} = 2.17$), the framework predicts Type~A confirmation bias ($\Delta \leq 0$); the observed $\Delta = -4.41$\,pp confirms the prediction.

A shuffle control further confirms the structural nature of this bias: randomizing constraint--answer associations while preserving per-trace counts yields $\\text{BR}_\\text{null} \\approx \\text{BR}_\\text{obs}$ ($p > 0.7$), indicating that BR reflects the trace-count distribution rather than extraction-specific error.\n\n\\looseness=-1 The pattern generalizes: $4.97\\times$ (Mistral), $3.85\\times$ (Qwen-14B), $2.88\\times$ (LLaMA), all $> 1.0$ with 95\\% bootstrap CIs (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). Cross-model extraction confirms universality across nine models from 7B to frontier (BR $1.66$--$4.97\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:frontier-scb}). BR $> 1$ further holds across all eight cells of a $2{\\times}2$ generator${\\times}$extractor matrix on FOLIO and ProofWriter (range $1.94$--$2.70$; Appendix~\\ref{app:2x2}). Constraints use trace-specific variables (56.4\\% zero cross-answer overlap), collapsing aggregation to majority voting.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\caption{Symbolic confirmation bias across nine models on FOLIO ($n = 204$). BR: mean per-problem bias ratio. $n_\\text{err}$: number of incorrect instances on which BR is computed. $\\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). Ext.: $^a$self-extraction; $^b$cross-model (Mistral-7B extracts FOL from target traces). $^*$Interim ($n = 95/204$). All models exhibit $\\text{BR} > 1$; BR estimates for high-accuracy models ($n_\\text{err} < 40$) have wider confidence intervals; Gemini ($n_\\text{err} \\approx 12$) should be interpreted with particular caution. Process-level similarity ($\\kappa_{\\text{proc}}$) ranges from $0.25$ (R1-Distill) to $0.79$ (gpt-4o/4.1/Gemini); see Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}.}\n\\label{tab:frontier-scb}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrrl@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Ext. & SC (\\%) & $n_\\text{err}$ & BR & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nMistral-7B & $^a$ & 55.88 & 90 & 4.97 & 0.00 \\\\\nLLaMA-8B & $^a$ & \\textrm{---} & \\textrm{---} & 2.88 & \\textrm{---} \\\\\nQwen-14B & $^a$ & 75.98 & 49 & 3.85 & $-$0.49 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nR1-Distill-8B & $^b$ & 74.51 & 52 & 2.92 & $+$2.45 \\\\\ngpt-4o & $^a$ & 73.91 & 53 & 2.94 & $-$1.80 \\\\\ngpt-4.1 & $^b$ & 82.84 & 35 & 2.70 & $-$1.47 \\\\\ngpt-5.5 & $^b$ & 84.31 & 32 & 3.48 & $+$0.49 \\\\\no3 & $^b$ & 82.65 & 35 & 1.66 & 0.00 \\\\\nGemini-2.5-Pro$^*$ & $^b$ & 87.37 & 12 & 2.79 & $-$1.10 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\looseness=-1 Two controls rule out answer leakage. BR is uniform across early, mid, and late reasoning steps (Fisher $p = 0.71$); high-consensus errors amplify to $\\text{BR} = 18.8\\times$ versus $2.7\\times$ for low-consensus ($p < 0.001$). Removing the answer sentence from traces before extraction yields no BR decrease (Wilcoxon $p \\geq 0.12$) despite significant accuracy loss (McNemar $p < 0.013$). Symbolic confirmation bias reflects a distributional prior embedded throughout reasoning, not answer contamination.\n\n\\label{sec:process-kappa}\n\\looseness=-1 Reasoning-process overlap exceeds answer-level correlation: on Mistral-7B FOLIO, mean process similarity (pairwise TF-IDF cosine; Appendix~\\ref{app:process-gradient}) reaches 0.69, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.46$. The gap generalizes across four families (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}). Under cross-model extraction, SICA gain decreases monotonically with process similarity ($\\rho = -1.0$, $p = 0.008$; Table~\\ref{tab:process-gradient}). Conditional correction analysis confirms the same pattern: cross-architecture NLI yields positive correction ratios while same-model SICA yields negative ones, with correction ratio correlating with combo gain ($\\rho = 0.84$, $p = 0.014$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cil}).\n\n\\looseness=-1 These patterns---universal BR $> 1$, structural equivalence to trace-count distributions, and process-level correlation exceeding answer-level agreement---follow from a structural property of self-extraction pipelines.\n\n\\medskip\\noindent\n\\textbf{Proposition 1} \\textit{(Symbolic Confirmation Bias is Structural).}\\;%\nLet $t_1, \\ldots, t_K \\overset{\\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\\sim} P_M(\\cdot \\mid q)$ be reasoning traces from model $M$ on question $q$.\nAssume:\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=\\textbf{A\\arabic*.},leftmargin=2.5em,nosep]\n\\item Answers are trace-determined: $a_i = h(t_i)$.\n\\item Constraints are trace-determined: $c_i = f(t_i)$.\n\\end{enumerate}\nLet $\\hat{a} = \\mathrm{mode}(a_1, \\ldots, a_K)$, $n_a = |\\{i : a_i = a\\}|$, and $Y$ the ground truth. Then:\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=\\textit{(\\roman*)},leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt,topsep=3pt]\n\\item \\textit{Voting degeneration.}\\;\nUnder source attribution (Eq.~\\ref{eq:score}), the constraint score reduces to the majority-vote count: $\\operatorname{score}(a) = n_a$.\nWhen $|\\{a : n_a > 0\\}| \\geq 2$, the bias ratio satisfies $\\mathrm{BR} \\triangleq n_{\\hat{a}} \\big/ \\max_{a' \\neq \\hat{a}} n_{a'} \\geq 1$.\n\n\\item \\textit{Information ceiling.}\\;\n$I(C_{1:K};\\, Y) \\leq I(T_{1:K};\\, Y)$ and $I(C_{1:K};\\, Y \\mid A_{1:K}) \\leq I(T_{1:K};\\, Y \\mid A_{1:K})$:\nself-extracted constraints cannot carry more information about $Y$ than the source traces, even beyond what the answers already encode.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\\noindent\nBoth bounds hold for \\emph{any} model $M$, task $q$, and extraction function $f$.\n\n\\medskip\n\\textit{Proof.}\\;%\n\\textit{(i)}\\; By definition, $\\operatorname{score}(a) = \\sum_{i=1}^K \\mathbf{1}[a_i = a] = n_a$, maximized at $\\hat{a}$, so $\\mathrm{BR} \\geq 1$.\n\\textit{(ii)}\\; $c_i = f(t_i)$ makes $C_{1:K}$ a deterministic function of $T_{1:K}$, so $Y \\mathop{\\text{---}} T_{1:K} \\mathop{\\text{---}} C_{1:K}$ forms a Markov chain and the data processing inequality gives $I(C_{1:K}; Y) \\leq I(T_{1:K}; Y)$.\nThe conditional bound follows identically: $c_i = f(t_i)$ holds unconditionally, so the Markov property and DPI apply within each stratum $\\{A_{1:K} = \\mathbf{a}\\}$.\\hfill$\\square$\n\n\\medskip\\noindent\nEach constraint is a downstream product of a trace that simultaneously determines an answer; constraint-based aggregation therefore cannot escape the distributional bias of the answer vote.\nNeither bound depends on model capability, task difficulty, or extraction quality. The bias is a structural artifact of the self-extraction pipeline.\n\n\\medskip\\noindent\n\\textbf{Remark.}\\;%\nPart~\\textit{(i)} relies on source attribution (Eq.~\\ref{eq:score}), the mechanism used by SICA.\nUnder \\emph{semantic} evaluation (checking whether $c_i$ is logically consistent with answer $a$ directly), $\\mathrm{BR} \\geq 1$ additionally requires \\emph{answer alignment}: $P(c_i \\text{ supports } a_i) \\geq P(c_i \\text{ supports } a')$ for $a' \\neq a_i$.\nThis property is structurally motivated (the reasoning chain producing $a_i$ generates intermediate steps aligned with $a_i$) and is empirically validated across all nine model--task conditions ($\\mathrm{BR} \\in [1.66,\\, 4.97]$; Table~\\ref{tab:frontier-scb}), but is not a mathematical necessity of self-extraction alone; it could fail if $f$ systematically extracted premises that contradict their source trace's conclusion.\nUnder the degeneration condition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}), which holds on 94.4\\% of problems (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}), these conclusions extend to the complete SICA scoring function.\n\nUnder SICA's source attribution (Eq.~\\ref{eq:score}), Proposition~1\\textit{(i)} implies $\\mathrm{BR} \\geq 1$ deterministically for every problem; the empirical BR (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mirage}), which evaluates semantic support across incorrect problems, consistently exceeds this structural bound ($1.66\\times$--$4.97\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:frontier-scb}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  node distance=0.8cm,\n  nd/.style={rectangle, rounded corners=2pt, draw=black!50,\n             fill=gray!6, minimum height=5.5mm, minimum width=8mm,\n             font=\\small},\n  bn/.style={nd, fill=red!10, draw=red!50, line width=0.7pt},\n  ex/.style={nd, draw=gray!50, densely dashed, fill=white},\n  >=Stealth,\n  base/.style={->, semithick, black!50},\n  leak/.style={->, line width=0.85pt, red!50},\n  sub/.style={font=\\scriptsize, text=gray!55},\n]\n\\node[nd]             (M) {$M$};\n\\node[nd, right=of M] (T) {$T$};\n\\node[bn, right=of T] (A) {$A$};\n\\node[nd, right=of A] (C) {$C$};\n\\node[nd, right=of C] (S) {$S$};\n\\node[ex, above=0.65cm of A] (Y) {$Y$};\n\n\\node[sub, below=0.5mm of M] {Model};\n\\node[sub, below=0.5mm of T] {Trace};\n\\node[sub, below=0.5mm of A] {Answer};\n\\node[sub, below=0.5mm of C] {Constr.};\n\\node[sub, below=0.5mm of S] {SICA};\n\\node[sub, above=0.5mm of Y] {Truth};\n\n\\draw[base] (M) -- (T);\n\\draw[leak] (T) -- (A);\n\\draw[leak] (A) -- (C);\n\\draw[base] (C) -- (S);\n\\draw[base, bend right=22] (T) to (C);\n\n\\draw[densely dashed, gray!30, shorten >=1pt, shorten <=1pt] (Y) -- (A);\n\n\\node[font=\\tiny, text=red!45, fill=white, inner sep=0.5pt]\n  at ($(A)!0.5!(C) + (0, 0.27)$) {\\textit{bottleneck}};\n\n\\node[draw=gray!30, rounded corners=1.5pt, fill=white,\n      inner sep=2.5pt, font=\\scriptsize, text=gray!50,\n      right=0.2cm of Y]\n  {$C \\!\\perp\\! Y \\mid A$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}\n\\caption{Causal DAG of the self-extraction pipeline. All information about ground truth $Y$ reaching constraints $C$ is mediated by answer $A$; Proposition~1\\textit{(ii)} formalizes this as an information ceiling via the data processing inequality.}\n\\label{fig:causal-dag}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\looseness=-1 Since the bias holds for any model, task, and extraction function $f$, training-free remediation strategies face a structural limitation: methods that preserve extraction accuracy necessarily inherit the model's distributional prior.\n\n\\subsection{Why Remediation Fails}\n\\label{sec:remediation}\n\\label{sec:interventions}\n\n\\looseness=-1 All 27 training-free remediation strategies spanning four categories produce neutral-to-negative results (Figure~\\ref{fig:interventions-summary}; Appendices~\\ref{app:extraction}--\\ref{app:entropy}). The pattern reveals a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off: methods that successfully break trace correlation simultaneously destroy accuracy, while methods that preserve accuracy fail to achieve independence.\n\n\\looseness=-1 Four representative failure modes illustrate this trade-off. \\emph{Debate} ($\\kappa = 0.15$, $\\Delta = -18.6$\\,pp) successfully breaks trace correlation but collapses accuracy: same-model diversity and quality are at odds. \\emph{Cross-model extraction} (using a different LLM as extractor) decreases $\\kappa$ slightly but $\\Delta \\approx 0$, because same-family models share systematic biases (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}). \\emph{Contrastive prompting} produces imbalanced output (94 pro vs.\\ 65 con constraints), indicating confirmation bias transfers to constraint generation. \\emph{Iterative constraint generation}, which refines constraints across multiple extraction rounds, reduces surface-level duplication but the refined constraints still align with the model's majority answer.\n\n\\looseness=-1 All non-debate strategies cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$, a same-model ceiling observed across three families. This ceiling is not a property of any single extraction method but of the model itself: regardless of prompting strategy, self-extraction cannot produce constraints that are independent of the model's distributional prior. The oracle ceiling ($+29.90$\\,pp with gold constraints) proves the mechanism is sound; self-extraction captures only ${\\sim}$12\\%, localizing the bottleneck to extraction quality rather than the MAX-SAT apparatus.\n\n\\looseness=-1 No individual strategy achieves significance after correction (Appendix~\\ref{app:intervention-individual}). The consistent failure across 27 strategies, four categories, and three model families suggests that symbolic confirmation bias is not a fixable extraction artifact but an intrinsic property of self-referential constraint generation.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_interventions_compact.pdf}\n\\caption{Twenty-three of 27 training-free strategies across four categories (four additional strategies tested but omitted for clarity). All produce neutral-to-negative $\\Delta$ vs.\\ SC. Grey band: $\\pm 1$\\,pp noise zone.}\n\\label{fig:interventions-summary}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Diagnostic Framework}\n\\label{sec:diagnostic-framework}\n\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n\n\\looseness=-1 Three zero-cost diagnostics predict the outcome of any trace-aggregation method from a $K = 12$ pilot run: (1) the degenerate-regime indicator $C_0 < 5\\%$ (SICA reduces to SC); (2) the diversity floor $K_\\text{eff} \\leq 1.3$ (insufficient vote diversity for any aggregation gain); (3) the bias ratio BR combined with SC regime (Type~A: SC $\\geq 60\\%$ with BR $> 1$ predicts confirmation-bias degradation; Type~C: SC $\\leq 40\\%$ predicts error amplification). These boundaries correctly classify all 60 tested conditions across four model families and three datasets.\n\n\\looseness=-1 Decision boundaries ($C_0 < 5\\%$, $K_\\text{eff} \\leq 1.3$, SC $< 40\\%$) were derived from Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-14B conditions. We evaluate prospectively on held-out LLaMA-3.1-8B, a different model family not used in boundary derivation: on FOLIO (SC $= 66.18\\%$, $C_0 = 61.8\\%$, $K_\\text{eff} = 2.17$), the framework predicts Type~A confirmation bias ($\\Delta \\leq 0$); the observed $\\Delta = -4.41$\\,pp confirms the prediction.",

50	\looseness=-1 Figure~\ref{fig:regime-scatter} reveals the regime structure underlying these results. Fleiss' $\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82 across conditions, yielding $K_{\text{eff}} = 1.20\text{--}3.62$ at $K = 12$ (Table~\ref{tab:independence}, Appendix). Positive $\Delta$ requires both sufficient $K_{\text{eff}}$ ($\leq 1.30$ never improves) and reasonable SC accuracy ($K_{\text{eff}} = 3.62$ with SC $= 39\%$ degrades rather than helps). Three regimes emerge: Type~C (SC $\leq 40\%$), where low base accuracy causes error amplification; an intermediate band (50--60\%), containing the only positive trends; and Type~A (SC $\geq 60\%$), where symbolic confirmation bias dominates. Seed-level noise is substantial: across three Mistral-7B seeds, $\kappa \in [0.41, 0.51]$ yet $\Delta$ ranges from $+2.94$ to $-2.45$\,pp, indicating that constraint quality varies beyond what independence alone predicts. We trace the failure mechanism in \S\ref{sec:analysis}.
51	
52	\looseness=-1 TOST equivalence tests \citep{lakens2017equivalence} with margin $\pm 2$\,pp confirm four conditions as equivalent; ten remain underpowered for effects below ${\sim}2$\,pp (MDE 1.86--4.84\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:power}). Absence of evidence is not evidence of absence for small effects. Three-seed replication on Mistral-7B confirms seed-level noise (mean $\Delta = +0.16 \pm 2.70$\,pp; Table~\ref{tab:multi-seed}). Four-seed replication on Qwen2.5-14B $\times$ FOLIO confirms the pattern ($\Delta \in [0.00, -2.01]$\,pp).
53	
54	\looseness=-1 Oracle controls replacing self-extracted constraints with gold annotations confirm the MAX-SAT mechanism is sound ($+29.90$/$+13.73$\,pp FOLIO, $+17.17$\,pp ProofWriter; $p < 0.001$), yet self-extraction captures only ${\sim}$12\% of this ceiling (Appendix~\ref{app:oracle}).

Degenerate conditions ($C_0 < 5\\%$: QwQ-32B AIME, Qwen2.5-14B GSM8K) yield $\\Delta = 0$; oracle upper bounds are reported in Appendix~\\ref{app:oracle}.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrrl@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Domain & $n$ & SC (\\%) & SICA (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Type C: error amplification (SC $\\leq$ 40\\%)}} \\\\\nMistral-7B & PW-D5 & 600 & 39.33 & 36.50 & $\\mathbf{-2.83}$ & 0.014{*} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Intermediate SC (SC $\\sim$50--60\\%, Mistral-7B only)}} \\\\\nMistral-7B & LogiQA & 200 & 50.00 & 51.50 & $+$1.50 & 0.581 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO (s123) & 204 & 54.41 & 57.35 & $+$2.94 & 0.070 \\\\\nMistral-7B$^{3s}$ & FOLIO & 204 & 56.53 & 56.70 & $+$0.16 & 1.000 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO (s456) & 204 & 55.88 & 55.88 & 0.00 & 1.000 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO (s789) & 204 & 59.31 & 56.86 & $-$2.45 & 0.267 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Type A: confirmation bias (SC $\\geq$ 60\\%)}} \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & FOLIO & 204 & 66.18 & 61.76 & $-$4.41 & 0.093 \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & StrategyQA & 200 & 72.00 & 74.00 & $+$2.00 & 0.424 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO (ICL)$^b$ & 199 & 74.37 & 74.87 & $+$0.50 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & PW-D5 & 600 & 74.00 & 71.50 & $\\mathbf{-2.50}$ & 0.011{*} \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B$^{4s}$ & FOLIO & 204 & 75.01 & 73.78 & $-$1.24 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 204 & 75.98 & 76.47 & $+$0.49 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & LogiQA & 200 & 79.00 & 78.00 & $-$1.00 & 0.625 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nQwen3-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 83.33 & 84.80 & $+$1.47 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen3-14B & PW-D5 & 600 & 85.83 & 85.67 & $-$0.17 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen3-14B (think) & FOLIO & 204 & 86.27 & 86.76 & $+$0.49 & 1.000 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{f2_regime_scatter}\n\\caption{SICA $\\Delta$ (pp) vs.\\ SC baseline. Filled: $n \\geq 100$; open: $n < 100$. The intermediate-SC region (50--60\\%, shaded) contains the only positive trends (none reaching $p < 0.05$). Type~C: nominally significant degradation (raw $p = 0.014$; BH adj.\\ $p = 0.112$).}\n\\label{fig:regime-scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\looseness=-1 Figure~\\ref{fig:regime-scatter} reveals the regime structure underlying these results. Fleiss' $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82 across conditions, yielding $K_{\\text{eff}} = 1.20\\text{--}3.62$ at $K = 12$ (Table~\\ref{tab:independence}, Appendix). Positive $\\Delta$ requires both sufficient $K_{\\text{eff}}$ ($\\leq 1.30$ never improves) and reasonable SC accuracy ($K_{\\text{eff}} = 3.62$ with SC $= 39\\%$ degrades rather than helps). Three regimes emerge: Type~C (SC $\\leq 40\\%$), where low base accuracy causes error amplification; an intermediate band (50--60\\%), containing the only positive trends; and Type~A (SC $\\geq 60\\%$), where symbolic confirmation bias dominates. Seed-level noise is substantial: across three Mistral-7B seeds, $\\kappa \\in [0.41, 0.51]$ yet $\\Delta$ ranges from $+2.94$ to $-2.45$\\,pp, indicating that constraint quality varies beyond what independence alone predicts. We trace the failure mechanism in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n\n\\looseness=-1 TOST equivalence tests \\citep{lakens2017equivalence} with margin $\\pm 2$\\,pp confirm four conditions as equivalent; ten remain underpowered for effects below ${\\sim}2$\\,pp (MDE 1.86--4.84\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:power}). Absence of evidence is not evidence of absence for small effects. Three-seed replication on Mistral-7B confirms seed-level noise (mean $\\Delta = +0.16 \\pm 2.70$\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:multi-seed}). Four-seed replication on Qwen2.5-14B $\\times$ FOLIO confirms the pattern ($\\Delta \\in [0.00, -2.01]$\\,pp).\n\n\\looseness=-1 Oracle controls replacing self-extracted constraints with gold annotations confirm the MAX-SAT mechanism is sound ($+29.90$/$+13.73$\\,pp FOLIO, $+17.17$\\,pp ProofWriter; $p < 0.001$), yet self-extraction captures only ${\\sim}$12\\% of this ceiling (Appendix~\\ref{app:oracle}).\n",

### Tool result

_source line 451 · 2026-05-23T05:02:22.426Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01DvRQKbDdQXjiZ4wAFdSdgp`</summary>

```text
1	Can symbolic verification via a provably sound solver improve Large Language Model (LLM) reasoning when constraints are self-extracted from the model's own traces? Despite Z3 validating 99\% of such constraints, the answer is no. We introduce Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts logical constraints from chain-of-thought traces and selects answers via weighted MAX-SAT, yet produces answers identical to Self-Consistency (SC) majority voting on 94.4\% of problems. Across six models (7B--32B), three reasoning domains, and over 3{,}000 condition-evaluations, no condition shows a significant positive effect after FDR control; four conditions confirm equivalence within $\pm$2\,pp, while ten remain underpowered for effects below ${\sim}$2\,pp. We trace this failure to \emph{symbolic confirmation bias}: on incorrect problems, 83.3\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer, with bias ratios from $1.66\times$ to $4.97\times$ across nine models from 7B to frontier scale. A shuffle control confirms this bias is structural ($p > 0.7$) and extends the self-correction impossibility of \citet{huang2024selfcorrect} to formal verification. All twenty-seven training-free remediation strategies fail due to a persistent independence-accuracy trade-off. Preliminary evidence suggests that structurally independent cross-architecture NLI verification can escape the bias ($+6.67$\,pp on the weakest generator, $p < 0.001$). Three zero-cost diagnostics ($K_\text{eff}$, BR, $C_0$) correctly classify all 60 tested conditions and allow practitioners to predict a priori whether symbolic aggregation will help.
2	
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	\looseness=-1
4	Can a provably sound formal solver improve LLM reasoning if the constraints it operates on are self-extracted from the model's own traces? We show that the answer is no: Z3 validates 99\% of self-extracted constraints, yet MAX-SAT produces answers identical to majority voting on 94.4\% of problems. The formal verification layer is vacuous, not because the solver fails, but because extraction inherits the model's distributional bias. This finding challenges a natural extension of Self-Consistency (SC) \citep{wang2023sc}, which samples $K$ chain-of-thought traces and selects the majority answer. SC's gains plateau and decline for strong models \citep{loo2025reevaluating}; the gap between its theoretical promise (independent voters converge to truth \citep{condorcet1785, ladha1992correlated}) and practice points to correlated errors that majority voting cannot correct.
5	
6	\looseness=-1
7	We study this through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts logical constraints from reasoning traces and selects answers via weighted partial MAX-SAT (\S\ref{sec:pipeline}; Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline}). Across six models (7B--32B), three reasoning domains, and over 3{,}000 condition-evaluations with multi-seed replication, no condition shows a significant positive effect after FDR control (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}). Four conditions are confirmed equivalent within $\pm$2\,pp; ten remain underpowered for effects below ${\sim}$2\,pp.
8	
9	\looseness=-1
10	The failure traces to \emph{symbolic confirmation bias}: on problems where the model errs, 83.3\% of self-extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer (bias ratio $1.66$--$4.97\times$ across nine models from 7B to frontier; \S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}). A shuffle control confirms this bias is structural, proportional to the trace-count distribution rather than extraction-specific error ($p > 0.7$). Prior work shows LLMs cannot self-correct without external feedback \citep{huang2024selfcorrect}; we demonstrate that this limitation extends to formal self-verification: even with a provably correct solver, self-extracted constraints carry no information beyond majority voting.
11	
12	\looseness=-1
13	Twenty-seven training-free remediation strategies produce neutral-to-negative results (\S\ref{sec:remediation}), revealing a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off. Cross-architecture verification provides preliminary evidence of escaping the bottleneck: a structurally independent NLI verifier yields $+6.67$\,pp on the weakest generator ($p < 0.001$); domain-adapted verification extends gains to generators with SC up to 72\% with monotonically decreasing benefit (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}). Three zero-cost diagnostics ($K_\text{eff}$, BR, $C_0$) correctly classify all 60 tested conditions (\S\ref{sec:diagnostic-framework}).
14	
15	Our contributions are:
16	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
17	\item \textbf{The symbolic mirage.} Provably sound Z3 verification becomes vacuous under self-extraction: symbolic confirmation bias (BR $1.66$--$4.97\times$ across nine models) renders MAX-SAT equivalent to majority voting on 94.4\% of problems. We prove this degeneration is structural (Proposition~1) and confirm it empirically via a shuffle control and 27 failed remediation strategies (\S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}, \S\ref{sec:remediation}).
18	
19	\item \textbf{Diagnostic framework.} Three zero-cost diagnostics ($K_\text{eff}$, BR, $C_0$) correctly classify all 60 tested conditions, providing practitioners with a priori prediction of symbolic aggregation viability from a $K{=}12$ pilot run (\S\ref{sec:diagnostic-framework}).
20	
21	\item \textbf{Cross-architecture escape.} Preliminary evidence that structurally independent NLI verification escapes the bottleneck ($+6.67$\,pp on the weakest generator, $p < 0.001$), with gain decreasing monotonically as generator accuracy increases (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}).
22	\end{enumerate}
23	
24	\begin{figure}[t]
25	\centering
26	\small
27	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{\begin{tikzpicture}[

1	\vspace{-2mm}
2	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	\looseness=-1 We identify symbolic confirmation bias: self-extracted constraints inherit the generating model's biases ($\text{BR} = 4.97\times$, 83.3\%), explaining why twenty-seven remediation strategies fail. Oracle controls ($+29.90$/$+13.73$\,pp) confirm the mechanism is sound; the bottleneck is extraction quality, not the formal channel. Three diagnostics predict failure ($C_0 < 5\%$, SC $\leq 40\%$, process similarity). Cross-architecture NLI escapes the bias: zero-shot gains for the weakest generator ($p < 0.001$); domain adaptation extends to generators with SC up to 72\%. DRC recovers 83--90\% of full-combo gain at 35--39\% cost.
5

```text
1	\section{Escaping the Bottleneck}
2	\label{sec:solutions}
3	\label{sec:cross-arch}
4	
5	\paragraph{Motivation.}
6	\looseness=-1 No training-free self-extraction method achieves independence from the model's distributional prior (\S\ref{sec:remediation}). We test whether a structurally independent verifier, sharing neither architecture nor training data with the generator, provides corrective signal that escapes the same-model ceiling.
7	
8	\paragraph{Method.}
9	\looseness=-1 We use DeBERTa-large \citep{he2021deberta} (406M, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \citep{williams2018mnli}, to classify (premise, conclusion) pairs as entailment, contradiction, or neutral. In combo mode, each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times alongside SC's $K$ votes. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$) and ProofWriter depth-5 ($n = 600$).
10	
11	\paragraph{Zero-shot results.}
12	\looseness=-1 DeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\% on FOLIO and 55.00\% on ProofWriter. Combo $w{=}3$ yields $+2.94$\,pp on FOLIO (n.s.) and $+6.67$\,pp on ProofWriter for Mistral-7B ($p < 0.001$, significant after BH correction, $m{=}12$); all generators with SC $\geq 72\%$ show no significant improvement ($\Delta \leq +0.83$\,pp, $p > 0.4$). Same-model SICA \emph{degrades} ProofWriter ($-2.83$\,pp; Table~\ref{tab:main_results}), illustrating that corrective signal must originate outside the model's distribution. Preliminary evidence suggests that zero-shot NLI escape requires the generator's SC to fall below the verifier's standalone accuracy.
13	
14	\paragraph{Capability gap.}
15	\looseness=-1 Four generators spanning a 47-point SC range on ProofWriter ($n{=}600$), all paired with DeBERTa-large-mnli (55.00\% standalone; Table~\ref{tab:cross_generator_nli}): only Mistral-7B (SC 39.33\%, below the verifier) gains significantly ($+6.67$\,pp, $p < 0.001$); all three generators above the verifier (SC $\geq 72\%$) show no significant improvement ($\Delta \leq +0.83$\,pp). The pattern is consistent with a capability-gap model: combo gain decreases monotonically with generator SC, with breakeven near the verifier's standalone accuracy. The monotonic relationship holds across three NLI architectures and six verifier-generator pairs (Spearman $\rho = 1.0$, $p = 0.003$, $n{=}6$; Figure~\ref{fig:capability-gap}; Appendix~\ref{app:capability-gap}). Per-label analysis confirms complementary error patterns that diminish as the generator improves (Appendix~\ref{app:multi-nli}).
16	
17	\begin{table}[t]
18	\centering
19	\small
20	\caption{Cross-generator NLI escape with DeBERTa-large-mnli verifier (55.00\% standalone, ProofWriter $n{=}600$). Combo gain decreases monotonically with generator capability. Only Mistral-7B (SC below the verifier) shows a significant gain.}
21	\label{tab:cross_generator_nli}
22	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccc@{}}
23	\toprule
24	Generator & Family & SC & Gap & Combo $\Delta$ ($w{=}3$) & $p$ \\
25	\midrule
26	Mistral-7B & Mistral & 39.33\% & $+$15.67 & $+$6.67\,pp & $<$0.001 \\
27	LLaMA-3.1-8B & LLaMA & 72.50\% & $-$17.50 & $+$0.83\,pp & 0.665 \\
28	Qwen2.5-14B & Qwen & 74.00\% & $-$19.00 & $+$0.67\,pp & 0.503 \\
29	Qwen3-14B & Qwen & 85.83\% & $-$30.83 & $+$0.50\,pp & 0.45 \\
30	\bottomrule
31	\end{tabular}}
32	\end{table}
33	
34	\paragraph{Domain-adapted verifier.}
35	\looseness=-1 Fine-tuning DeBERTa on ProofWriter depth-5 OWA (99.67\% standalone) amplifies the escape: combo gains reach $+$12.67\,pp (Mistral-7B, $w{=}3$) and $+$11.67\,pp (LLaMA-8B), a $14\times$ amplification over LLaMA's zero-shot result ($+0.83 \to +11.67$\,pp), with near-total error complementarity ($364{:}2$ and $164{:}1$; Table~\ref{tab:finetuned-nli}, Appendix). Domain-adapted verification extends escape to generators with SC up to approximately 72\%, with monotonically decreasing gain, consistent with the prediction that the capability gap can be shifted by increasing verifier accuracy. A cost-aware cascade recovers 83--90\% of full-combo gain at ${\sim}35\%$ NLI cost (Appendix~\ref{app:drc}).
36

```text
1178	We recommend four zero-cost diagnostics, computable from sampled traces alone, to predict whether symbolic self-verification will help or hurt before deployment.
1179	\emph{Bias Ratio} (BR; \S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}): for each problem, compute the fraction of constraints supporting the majority answer, divided by majority vote share. $\text{BR} > 1$ indicates constraints are biased toward the majority; $\text{BR} \gg 2$ (as in all nine models tested; Table~\ref{tab:frontier-scb}) signals that extraction amplifies rather than checks the model's prior. Report as mean $\pm$ bootstrap 95\% CI.
1180	\emph{Effective-K} ($K_\text{eff}$; Eq.~\ref{eq:effk}): compute Fleiss' $\kappa$ across $K$ traces, then $K_\text{eff} = 1 + (K-1)(1-\kappa)$. Threshold: $K_\text{eff} \leq 1.3$ never improves in our experiments---aggregation collapses to majority voting. $K_\text{eff} > 1.8$ is a necessary (not sufficient) condition for gain.
1181	\emph{Degenerate Consensus} ($C_0$; \S\ref{sec:condorcet}): the fraction of problems where all $K$ traces agree. $C_0 > 95\%$ renders aggregation moot; $C_0 < 5\%$ means the model is maximally uncertain and constraints tend to amplify noise.
1182	\emph{ProcessSim} (Eq.~\ref{eq:process-sim}): mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace vectors. $\text{ProcessSim} > 0.6$ indicates homogeneous reasoning; combined with $\text{BR} > 2$, this predicts SCB will dominate.
1183	A practitioner should compute all four on a held-out sample ($n \geq 50$). If $K_\text{eff} \leq 1.3$ or $C_0 > 95\%$ or ($\text{BR} > 2$ and $\text{ProcessSim} > 0.6$), symbolic self-verification is unlikely to help.
1184	
1185	\paragraph{Future directions: trained extractors and beyond.}
1186	\label{app:future-directions}
1187	Our oracle controls reveal that perfect extraction would yield $+29.90$\,pp over SC on Mistral-7B FOLIO---self-extraction captures only ${\sim}12\%$ of this ceiling. This gap motivates several future directions.
```

    "newString": "$K_\\text{eff} \\leq 1.30$ never improves in our experiments---aggregation collapses to majority voting. $K_\\text{eff} > 1.8$",
    "originalFile": null,
    "structuredPatch": [

        "oldStart": 1177,

        "lines": [
          " \\label{app:diagnostic-toolkit}",
          " We recommend four zero-cost diagnostics, computable from sampled traces alone, to predict whether symbolic self-verification will help or hurt before deployment.",
          " \\emph{Bias Ratio} (BR; \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}): for each problem, compute the fraction of constraints supporting the majority answer, divided by majority vote share. $\\text{BR} > 1$ indicates constraints are biased toward the majority; $\\text{BR} \\gg 2$ (as in all nine models tested; Table~\\ref{tab:frontier-scb}) signals that extraction amplifies rather than checks the model's prior. Report as mean $\\pm$ bootstrap 95\\% CI.",
          "-\\emph{Effective-K} ($K_\\text{eff}$; Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}): compute Fleiss' $\\kappa$ across $K$ traces, then $K_\\text{eff} = 1 + (K-1)(1-\\kappa)$. Threshold: $K_\\text{eff} \\leq 1.3$ never improves in our experiments---aggregation collapses to majority voting. $K_\\text{eff} > 1.8$ is a necessary (not sufficient) condition for gain.",
          "+\\emph{Effective-K} ($K_\\text{eff}$; Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}): compute Fleiss' $\\kappa$ across $K$ traces, then $K_\\text{eff} = 1 + (K-1)(1-\\kappa)$. Threshold: $K_\\text{eff} \\leq 1.30$ never improves in our experiments---aggregation collapses to majority voting. $K_\\text{eff} > 1.8$ is a necessary (not sufficient) condition for gain.",
          " \\emph{Degenerate Consensus} ($C_0$; \\S\\ref{sec:condorcet}): the fraction of problems where all $K$ traces agree. $C_0 > 95\\%$ renders aggregation moot; $C_0 < 5\\%$ means the model is maximally uncertain and constraints tend to amplify noise.",
          " \\emph{ProcessSim} (Eq.~\\ref{eq:process-sim}): mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace vectors. $\\text{ProcessSim} > 0.6$ indicates homogeneous reasoning; combined with $\\text{BR} > 2$, this predicts SCB will dominate.",
          " A practitioner should compute all four on a held-out sample ($n \\geq 50$). If $K_\\text{eff} \\leq 1.3$ or $C_0 > 95\\%$ or ($\\text{BR} > 2$ and $\\text{ProcessSim} > 0.6$), symbolic self-verification is unlikely to help."
        ]
      }
    ],
